\title{Infinitely Many Primes $p=m^2+n^2$ with $n<p^{0.1129}$}

\author{Xuda Ye}
\affil{Department of Mathematics, Purdue University, West Lafayette, IN 47907}
\date{\today}

\begin{document}
\maketitle

\begin{abstract}
We prove that infinitely many primes $p = m^2 + n^2$ have $n < p^{0.1129}$, and that infinitely many primes satisfy $\{\sqrt{p}\} < p^{-0.2742}$. This sharpens the exponents $0.119$ and $-0.262$ of Harman and Lewis (2001). Following their approach, we derive both statements from a lower bound of the correct order of magnitude for the number of Gaussian primes of norm between $X/2$ and $X$ in every sector of angle $X^{-0.3871}$, and we prove this bound with Harman's sieve and their arithmetic information. The new ingredients are three groupings of terms that their sieve discards: a factorization of a two-dimensional sum for the linear estimate, and two applications of the bilinear estimate to four-dimensional sums, one of them after a further application of Buchstab's identity. These groupings give the grouped terms asymptotic formulas, so that fewer terms are discarded. In addition, we give every step of the sieve argument in full, filling in the details that Harman and Lewis present briefly and making several of their statements and discarded regions precise, and we evaluate the losses of the discarded sums by two numerical methods. The same argument in $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{2}]$ gives infinitely many primes $p$ with $1 - \{\sqrt{p}\} < p^{-0.2742}$.
\end{abstract}

\textbf{Keywords:} Gaussian primes, narrow sectors, Harman's sieve, Hecke characters, fractional part of $\sqrt{p}$, real quadratic fields.

\textbf{2020 MSC:} 11N36, 11N32, 11R44, 11J71.

\section{Introduction}
\label{sec: introduction}

A prime $p \equiv 1 \pmod 4$ is the norm $p = m^2 + n^2$ of a Gaussian prime $m + in$, a prime of the ring $\mathbb{Z}[i]$ of Gaussian integers. Landau asked whether $n = 1$ occurs for infinitely many primes, that is, whether infinitely many primes have the form $m^2 + 1$ \citep{pintz2009landau}. The question is open. \citet{iwaniec1978almost} showed that infinitely many integers $m^2 + 1$ have at most $2$ prime factors. Infinitely many primes $p = m^2 + n^2$ are known to have $n$ in sparse sets of arithmetic form: the squares \citep{friedlander1998polynomial}, the squares of primes \citep{heathbrown2017prime}, and the integers $y^2 + 1$ \citep{merikoski2023polynomials}. These results restrict the form of $n$, not its size. A weaker form of Landau's question asks how small $n$ can be taken for infinitely many primes $p = m^2 + n^2$. With $m > n > 0$, the condition $n < p^{\theta}$ places the Gaussian prime $m + in$ inside a sector of angle about $p^{\theta - 1/2}$ next to the real axis. So the problem is one of Gaussian primes in narrow sectors.

Under the Riemann hypothesis for the Hecke $L$-functions of $\mathbb{Q}(i)$, \citet{ankeny1952representations} and \citet{kubilius1955problem} showed that infinitely many primes $p = m^2 + n^2$ have $n \ll \log p$. Without hypotheses, every sector of angle greater than $X^{-3/10 + \varepsilon}$, for every fixed $\varepsilon > 0$, contains the expected number of Gaussian primes of norm in $(X, 2X]$, by a result of \citet{ricci1976local} that \citet[Theorem~3]{coleman1993rosser} states for every imaginary quadratic field. This covers $n < p^{\theta}$ for every $\theta > 1/5$. For smaller $\theta$, \citet{coleman1993rosser} obtained $n < p^{0.1631}$ with the Rosser--Iwaniec sieve in number fields. \citet{harman2001gaussian} used essentially the arithmetic information of Coleman with Harman's sieve \citep{harman2007prime}, and obtained $n < p^{0.119}$. Among the results that hold for every sector, this bound has remained the best known \citep[\S1.2]{jarviniemi2024gaussian}. \citet{harman2004prime} apply the same sieve to sectors in which the norm also lies in a short interval. Results that hold for almost all sectors go much further. \citet{huang2020gaussian} prove an asymptotic formula for the number of Gaussian primes of norm in $(X, 2X]$ in almost all sectors of angle greater than $X^{-3/5 + \varepsilon}$, for every fixed $\varepsilon > 0$. \citet{jarviniemi2024gaussian} find products of exactly $2$ Gaussian primes in almost all sectors of area $(\log X)^{15.1}$ in the disc of radius $X^{1/2}$.

If $p = m^2 + n^2$ with positive integers $m, n$ and $n < p^{\theta}$, then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: fractional part}
0 < \sqrt{p} - m = \frac{n^2}{\sqrt{p} + m} < p^{2\theta - 1/2}.
\end{equation}
Let $\{x\}$ denote the fractional part of $x$. For $\theta < 1/4$, \eqref{eq: fractional part} gives $0 < \sqrt{p} - m < 1$, so
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: fractional bound}
\{\sqrt{p}\} = \sqrt{p} - m < p^{-(1/2 - 2\theta)}.
\end{equation}
Harman and Lewis deduced $\{\sqrt{p}\} < p^{-0.262}$ for infinitely many primes \citep[Theorem~4]{harman2001gaussian} from their bound $n < p^{0.119}$ \citep[Theorem~1]{harman2001gaussian}. Earlier, \citet{balog1985distribution} and \citet{harman1983distribution} had obtained the exponent $1/4 - \varepsilon$ for every fixed $\varepsilon > 0$. The exponent $0.262$ has remained the best known \citep{li2025remark}. No exponent above $1/2$ is possible in such a bound, since $|\sqrt{p} - k| \Ge 1/(2\sqrt{p} + 1)$ for the integer $k$ nearest to $\sqrt{p}$.

Our main result is the following.

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm: primes}
There are infinitely many primes $p = m^2 + n^2$ with $m, n$ positive integers and $n < p^{0.1129}$. Consequently, there are infinitely many primes $p$ with $\{\sqrt{p}\} < p^{-0.2742}$.
\end{theorem}

Theorem~\ref{thm: primes} follows from a lower bound for the number of Gaussian primes in narrow sectors. Here and below, the norm of a Gaussian integer $m + in$ is $|m + in|^2 = m^2 + n^2$. A non-zero Gaussian integer, and in particular a product of Gaussian integers, is identified with its associate of argument in $[0, \pi/2)$, and arguments are taken modulo $\pi/2$.

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm: sector}
Let $\theta = 0.1129$. There is a constant $c > 0$ such that, uniformly in $\psi \in \mathbb{R}$ and for every sufficiently large $X$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: sector bound}
\#\bigl\{ \varpi \in \mathbb{Z}[i] : \varpi \text{ Gaussian prime},\ X/2 \Le |\varpi|^2 < X,\ \psi \Le \arg \varpi < \psi + X^{\theta - 1/2} \bigr\} \Ge \frac{c X^{1/2 + \theta}}{\log X}.
\end{equation}
\end{theorem}

The sector contains about $X^{1/2 + \theta}/(\pi \log X)$ Gaussian primes of norm in $[X/2, X)$ on average over $\psi$, so the lower bound \eqref{eq: sector bound} has the correct order of magnitude. For every $\psi$, an upper bound of the same order follows from the linear upper-bound sieve over $\mathbb{Z}[i]$ \citep[Lemma~3]{coleman1993rosser}. The linear estimate of Lemma~\ref{lem: type I}(a), applied to the positive and the negative parts of the sieve weights, gives this sieve the level of distribution $X^{1/2 + \theta - o(1)}$. The sieve then bounds the number of Gaussian primes of norm in $[X/2, X)$ in the sector by $(4/(1 + 2\theta) + o(1))\, X^{1/2 + \theta}/(\pi \log X)$ uniformly in $\psi$.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm: primes} from Theorem~\ref{thm: sector}]
Let $\theta = 0.1129$, and apply \eqref{eq: sector bound} with $\psi = 0$ and $X = 2^k$ for large $k$. The Gaussian primes of argument $0$ are the rational primes $\ell \equiv 3 \pmod 4$, and fewer than $X^{1/2}$ of them have norm $\ell^2 < X$. Since $c X^{1/2 + \theta}/\log X > X^{1/2}$ for large $X$, the sector contains a Gaussian prime $\varpi = m + in$ with $0 < \arg \varpi < X^{\theta - 1/2}$, so that $m, n > 0$. Its norm $p = m^2 + n^2$ is a rational prime with $X/2 \Le p < X$. Since $\theta < 1/2$ and $p < X$,
\begin{equation*}
n = \sqrt{p}\, \sin(\arg \varpi) < \sqrt{p}\, X^{\theta - 1/2} < \sqrt{p}\, p^{\theta - 1/2} = p^{\theta}.
\end{equation*}
The ranges $[X/2, X)$ for different $k$ are disjoint. So the primes $p$ are distinct for different $k$, and there are infinitely many of them. The bound $\{\sqrt{p}\} < p^{-0.2742}$ follows from \eqref{eq: fractional bound}, because $1/2 - 2\theta = 0.2742$.
\end{proof}

The bound \eqref{eq: fractional bound} places $\sqrt{p}$ slightly above an integer. For every fixed $\varepsilon > 0$, \citet[Chapter~9]{harman1982diophantine} proves a lower bound for the number of primes in the intervals $[(n + h)^2, (n + h)^2 + n^{1/2 + \varepsilon}]$, $X^{1/2} \Le n \Le (2X)^{1/2}$, uniformly in $0 \Le h \Le 1$. With $h$ close to $1$, it gives infinitely many primes with $1 - \{\sqrt{p}\} < p^{-1/4 + \varepsilon}$, that is, primes slightly below a square. \citet[p.~120]{harman2001gaussian} note that their method, unlike those of Balog and Harman, gives no bound for $\{\sqrt{p} - \beta\}$ with $\beta \neq 0$. In $\mathbb{Z}[\sqrt{2}]$, the argument of this paper gives the following.

\begin{theorem}
\label{thm: below square}
There are infinitely many primes $p = m^2 - 2n^2$ with $m, n$ positive integers and $n < p^{0.1129}$. Consequently, there are infinitely many primes $p$ with $1 - \{\sqrt{p}\} < p^{-0.2742}$.
\end{theorem}

\subsection{Outline of the method}
\label{subsec: method}

The proof of Theorem~\ref{thm: sector} refines the argument of \citet{harman2001gaussian}. Let $\mathcal{A}$ be the set of Gaussian integers in the sector of \eqref{eq: sector bound} with norm in $[X/2, X)$, and let $\mathcal{B}$ be the set of all Gaussian integers of argument in $[0, \pi/2)$ with norm in $[X/2, X)$, so $\mathcal{A} \subset \mathcal{B}$. The number of Gaussian primes in $\mathcal{A}$ is the number of elements of $\mathcal{A}$ without prime factors of norm below $X^{1/2}$. Buchstab's identity \eqref{eq: buchstab} decomposes this count into sums of two kinds. Sums of the first kind have asymptotic formulas (Definition~\ref{def: asymptotic formula}): they equal $2X^{\theta - 1/2}/\pi$ times the corresponding sums for $\mathcal{B}$, up to a negligible error. Harman and Lewis derive these formulas from bounds for Hecke $L$-functions, through linear (Type~I) and bilinear (Type~II) estimates. A sum has such a formula when its primes can be combined into variables whose norms lie in the ranges that these estimates admit.

Sums of the second kind consist of non-negative terms that enter with the sign $+$, so a lower bound may omit them. They are discarded. Each discarded sum, taken over $\mathcal{B}$ and divided by the number of Gaussian primes in $\mathcal{B}$, tends to an integral \eqref{eq: loss} of Buchstab's function $\omega$ of \eqref{eq: buchstab function} over a region of exponents. This integral is the loss of that region (Definition~\ref{def: loss}), and the sum of these losses is the total loss $\Phi(\theta)$ (Definition~\ref{def: total loss}). Buchstab's identity decomposes the number of Gaussian primes in $\mathcal{B}$ into the same sums over $\mathcal{B}$. So, up to a negligible error, the number of Gaussian primes in $\mathcal{A}$ is at least $2X^{\theta - 1/2}/\pi$ times the difference between the number of Gaussian primes in $\mathcal{B}$ and the discarded sums over $\mathcal{B}$. With the total loss, this gives the lower bound
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: lower bound intro}
\#\{\varpi \in \mathcal{A} : \varpi \text{ Gaussian prime}\} \Ge \frac{2X^{\theta - 1/2}}{\pi}\, \#\{\varpi \in \mathcal{B} : \varpi \text{ Gaussian prime}\}\, \bigl(1 - \Phi(\theta) - o(1)\bigr).
\end{equation}
Since $\mathcal{B}$ contains $(1 + o(1))\, X/(2\log X)$ Gaussian primes by \eqref{eq: primes in B}, \eqref{eq: lower bound intro} gives \eqref{eq: sector bound} for every $\theta \in (1/9, 1/8)$ with $\Phi(\theta) < 1$. For their decomposition, Harman and Lewis state that the total loss at $\theta = 0.119$ is below $0.943$ \citep[p.~134]{harman2001gaussian}.

Besides the prime number theorem in progressions modulo $4$ \citep[Section~1.2]{thorner2024refinements}, only the arithmetic information is quoted without proof. It consists of the sector inequality, the mean-value estimate for Hecke characters, the prime number theorem with Hecke characters, and Lemmas~6 and~7 of Harman and Lewis. Lemmas~\ref{lem: sector inequality} to~\ref{lem: type I input} of Appendix~\ref{app: arithmetic} state them.

\subsection{Contributions}
\label{subsec: contributions}

We add three groupings of terms that Harman and Lewis discard. A \emph{grouping} combines the primes of a sum into the variables of the linear or bilinear estimate, after a further application of Buchstab's identity \eqref{eq: buchstab} where needed. The sum of the grouped terms then has an asymptotic formula.
\begin{itemize}
\setlength{\itemsep}{0pt}
\item \emph{A new factorization on a region $\mathsf{R}$.} Harman and Lewis discard the sum of the terms $pq$, with $p$ and $q$ Gaussian primes, whose exponents lie in a two-dimensional region $\mathsf{R}$. Two applications of Buchstab's identity introduce primes $r$ and $s$. We apply the linear estimate to the factorization $p \cdot (qr)$ of the sum over $(p, q, r)$. The sum over $(p, q, r, s)$ is discarded, except where the other two groupings apply. Harman and Lewis apply the linear estimate after Buchstab's identity only to the factorization $(pq) \cdot r$. On $\mathsf{R}$ the product $pq$ is too large for the linear estimate, while $p$ and $qr$ satisfy its conditions, because $q$ is small.
\item \emph{The bilinear estimate with any set of primes on a region $\mathsf{G}$.} The other two groupings act on terms $pqrst$ in which two applications of Buchstab's identity have introduced primes $r$ and $s$, and $t$ is the remaining factor, the cofactor. Harman and Lewis discard these terms over four-dimensional regions, except on a few subregions. The bilinear estimate needs a prime variable and further variables $u$ and $v$ of norm at least about $X^{1/2 - \theta}$, and it applies at every level of sifting of $t$. On a region $\mathsf{G}$, we apply it with $s$ as the prime variable and with the product of a non-empty subset of $\{p, q, r\}$ as $u$. Harman and Lewis print the subset $\{p, r\}$ in one of their regions, and omit the conditions from other groupings in another.
\item \emph{A further Buchstab step on a region $\mathsf{L}$.} On a region $\mathsf{L}$, we apply Buchstab's identity once more, at a level below every fixed positive power of $X$. This application introduces a prime $s'$ with $|s'| \Le |s|$. The linear estimate for $pqrs$ then treats the sum sifted at this level, and the bilinear estimate with $s'$ as its prime variable treats the sum over $s'$. Here $u$ may contain $s$, which on $\mathsf{G}$ is the prime variable.
\end{itemize}
Section~\ref{sec: outline} defines $\mathsf{R}$, $\mathsf{G}$ and $\mathsf{L}$ and places them in the decomposition.

Table~\ref{tab: roots} shows the effect of each grouping. With the regions derived from the lemmas of Section~\ref{sec: two primes} (Remark~\ref{rem: misprints}), the decomposition of Harman and Lewis has total loss $1$ at $\theta = 0.1167825$. The value $0.1167825$ is just below the range from $0.117$ to $0.118$ in which, by \citet[p.~134]{harman2001gaussian}, the constant in their lower bound ceases to be positive. They add that they did not spend much time on the numerical calculations. Adding $\mathsf{R}$ moves the value $0.1167825$ to $0.1141073$, and adding $\mathsf{G}$ and $\mathsf{L}$ moves it to $0.1128554$, so that the total loss is below $1$ at $\theta = 0.1129$. The losses are computed in floating point by two methods (Section~\ref{sec: numerics}). At $\theta = 0.1129$ the first method gives the total loss $0.9990033$. The gap $1 - 0.9990033 = 9.967 \times 10^{-4}$ is more than $800$ times each of the following: the difference between the totals of the methods, the error estimate of the second method, and the change of the total of the first method from the rule of Table~\ref{tab: losses} to its finest rule.

We also give in full several steps that \citet{harman2001gaussian} present briefly: the fundamental lemma invoked in their Lemmas~8, 9, 11 and~12, the proofs of their Lemmas~8 to~10, the removal of the conditions that couple the variables of their bilinear estimate, and the extension of their Lemma~14 that part~6 of their \S5 uses. Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental}, Lemmas~\ref{lem: level large} and~\ref{lem: level small} with Corollary~\ref{cor: level one}, Lemma~\ref{lem: decoupling} and Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 14} fill in these steps. Appendix~\ref{app: comparison} compares the two arguments item by item, including several statements and discarded regions that are made precise here.

\subsection{Structure of the paper}
\label{subsec: structure}

Section~\ref{sec: notation} fixes the notation. Section~\ref{sec: outline} states the decomposition: identity \eqref{eq: identity 37}, the ranges of Table~\ref{tab: ranges}, the groupings $\mathsf{R}$, $\mathsf{G}$ and $\mathsf{L}$, and the total loss \eqref{eq: total loss}. Section~\ref{sec: estimates} states the linear and bilinear estimates, a fundamental lemma over $\mathbb{Z}[i]$, and asymptotic formulas for sifting functions with a variable level, and Section~\ref{sec: decoupling} removes the conditions that couple the variables of a sum. Section~\ref{sec: two primes} treats the sums over two primes. Section~\ref{sec: decomposition} proves identity \eqref{eq: identity 37}, checks the hypotheses of the lemmas on each range of Table~\ref{tab: ranges}, and defines the discarded regions. Section~\ref{sec: four} treats the four-dimensional sums on $\mathsf{G}$ and $\mathsf{L}$. Section~\ref{sec: integrals} turns the discarded sums into integrals and proves the lower bound, Lemma~\ref{lem: lower bound}. Section~\ref{sec: numerics} evaluates the integrals numerically and proves Theorem~\ref{thm: sector}, and Section~\ref{sec: conclusion} concludes. Appendix~\ref{app: arithmetic} collects standard facts and derives the linear and bilinear estimates from the arithmetic information. Appendix~\ref{app: sieve} proves Lemmas~\ref{lem: fundamental}, \ref{lem: level large}, \ref{lem: level small} and~\ref{lem: decoupling}, and Appendix~\ref{app: integrals} proves Lemma~\ref{lem: boxes to integrals}. Appendix~\ref{app: inequalities} lists the inequalities between the parameters, Appendix~\ref{app: comparison} compares the argument with that of Harman and Lewis, and Appendix~\ref{app: sqrt2} proves Theorem~\ref{thm: below square}.

Theorem~\ref{thm: sector} follows from Lemma~\ref{lem: lower bound} and Table~\ref{tab: losses}. Lemma~\ref{lem: lower bound} rests on Lemmas~\ref{lem: lemma 11} to~\ref{lem: boxes to integrals}, and Lemmas~\ref{lem: lemma 11} to~\ref{lem: four dim} rest on the results of Sections~\ref{sec: estimates} and~\ref{sec: decoupling}.

\section{Notation}
\label{sec: notation}

Throughout, $1/9 < \theta < 1/8$, a range in which all the inequalities of Table~\ref{tab: inequalities} hold, and $X$ is large in terms of $\theta$ and of the other fixed parameters. Put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: parameters}
\begin{gathered}
\sigma := \frac12 - \theta, \quad \tau := \frac12 + \theta, \quad \rho := \frac{1 + 22\theta}{12}, \quad \zeta := \frac{1 + 4\theta}{3}, \quad \nu := \frac{7 + 10\theta}{12}, \\
\mu := \exp\bigl( (\log X)^{4/5} \bigr).
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
Here $\sigma$, $\tau$, $\rho$, $\zeta$ and $\nu$ are the exponents of \citet[p.~120]{harman2001gaussian}. They satisfy $\nu - \sigma = \rho$ and $\nu - \sigma/2 = \zeta$. The exponents $\tau$, $\zeta$, $\rho$ and $\nu$ bound, as powers of $X$, the norms of the variables of the linear estimate, Lemma~\ref{lem: type I}, and $X^{\sigma}$ bounds from below, up to a constant factor, the norms of the variables $u$ and $v$ of the bilinear estimate, Lemma~\ref{lem: type II}. The number $X^{-\sigma} = X^{\theta - 1/2}$ is the angle of the sector in \eqref{eq: sector bound}. The number $\mu$ exceeds every fixed power of $\log X$ and is smaller than every fixed positive power of $X$. The fundamental lemma, Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental}, sifts at levels up to $\mu^4$, and the prime variable of the bilinear estimate has norm at least $\mu^4/2$.

For a Gaussian integer $n$, $n \sim N$ means $N \Le |n|^2 < 2N$. From now on, $p, q, r, s$ and $\varpi$ denote Gaussian primes, $d(n)$ is the number of divisors of $n$, and $\mathbf{1}[\cdot]$ is the indicator of the condition in brackets. A sequence $(a_n)$ is \emph{divisor-bounded} if $|a_n| \Le d(n)^{C_0}$ for a fixed $C_0$. Fix $\psi \in \mathbb{R}$. The lower bounds for $X$ below do not depend on $\psi$. The sets $\mathcal{A}$ and $\mathcal{B}$ of Section~\ref{subsec: method} are
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: sets}
\mathcal{A} = \{ n \in \mathbb{Z}[i] : X/2 \Le |n|^2 < X,\ \psi \Le \arg n < \psi + X^{-\sigma} \}, \qquad \mathcal{B} = \{ n \in \mathbb{Z}[i] : X/2 \Le |n|^2 < X \}.
\end{equation}
For $\mathcal{E} \in \{\mathcal{A}, \mathcal{B}\}$ and a Gaussian integer $u \neq 0$, put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: multiples}
\mathcal{E}_u := \{ n \in \mathcal{E} : u \mid n \}.
\end{equation}

Buchstab's identity classifies a Gaussian integer by its least prime factor, so it needs a total order on the Gaussian primes. The norm does not give one, since a rational prime $\ell \equiv 1 \pmod 4$ is the norm of $2$ Gaussian primes. As in \citet[p.~122]{harman2001gaussian}, the Gaussian primes are ordered by norm, and primes of equal norm by argument:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: order}
\varpi \prec \varpi' \quad \text{means} \quad |\varpi| < |\varpi'|, \ \text{or} \ |\varpi| = |\varpi'| \text{ and } \arg \varpi < \arg \varpi'.
\end{equation}
Then $\varpi \preccurlyeq \varpi'$ means $\varpi \prec \varpi'$ or $\varpi = \varpi'$, and $\succ$ and $\succcurlyeq$ are the reversed relations.

\begin{definition}[Sifting functions]
\label{def: sifting}
For $\mathcal{E} \in \{\mathcal{A}, \mathcal{B}\}$, $z \Ge 1$ and a prime $q$, the \emph{sifting functions} of \citet[p.~121]{harman2001gaussian} are
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: sifting}
S(\mathcal{E}, z) := \#\{ n \in \mathcal{E} : |\varpi|^2 \Ge z \text{ for all } \varpi \mid n \}, \qquad S(\mathcal{E}, q) := \#\{ n \in \mathcal{E} : \varpi \succcurlyeq q \text{ for all } \varpi \mid n \},
\end{equation}
and $z$ and $q$ are their \emph{levels}. With $\mathcal{E}_u$ of \eqref{eq: multiples} in place of $\mathcal{E}$, \eqref{eq: sifting} defines $S(\mathcal{E}_u, z)$ and $S(\mathcal{E}_u, q)$. For $n \in \mathcal{E}_u$, the quotient $n/u$ is the \emph{cofactor} of $n$, and the functions that sift the cofactor alone are
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: cofactor sieve}
S'(\mathcal{E}_u, z) := \#\{ n \in \mathcal{E}_u : |\varpi|^2 \Ge z \text{ for all } \varpi \mid n/u \}, \qquad S'(\mathcal{E}_u, q) := \#\{ n \in \mathcal{E}_u : \varpi \succcurlyeq q \text{ for all } \varpi \mid n/u \}.
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

Using the letter $S$ for both functions of \eqref{eq: sifting} is a slight abuse of notation. It causes no ambiguity, since a prime level is always written as a symbol that denotes a Gaussian prime, such as $q$, $s'$ or $\varpi$, and every other level is a real number. By \eqref{eq: sifting} and \eqref{eq: cofactor sieve},
\begin{equation*}
\begin{gathered}
S(\mathcal{E}_u, z) = S'(\mathcal{E}_u, z) \ \text{if } |\varpi|^2 \Ge z \text{ for every } \varpi \mid u, \qquad S(\mathcal{E}_u, z) = 0 \ \text{otherwise}, \\
S(\mathcal{E}_u, q) = S'(\mathcal{E}_u, q) \ \text{if } \varpi \succcurlyeq q \text{ for every } \varpi \mid u, \qquad S(\mathcal{E}_u, q) = 0 \ \text{otherwise}.
\end{gathered}
\end{equation*}
Classifying $n/u$ by its least prime factor for the order \eqref{eq: order} gives Buchstab's identities
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: buchstab}
S'(\mathcal{E}_u, z) = S'(\mathcal{E}_u, w) - \sum_{w \Le |\varpi|^2 < z} S'(\mathcal{E}_{u\varpi}, \varpi), \qquad S'(\mathcal{E}_u, q) = S'(\mathcal{E}_u, w) - \sum_{\varpi \prec q,\ |\varpi|^2 \Ge w} S'(\mathcal{E}_{u\varpi}, \varpi),
\end{equation}
for $1 \Le w \Le z$ in the identity for $S'(\mathcal{E}_u, z)$ and for $1 \Le w \Le |q|^2$ in the identity for $S'(\mathcal{E}_u, q)$. These are \citet[(13)]{harman2001gaussian}, and the same identities hold for $S$ in place of $S'$ when $u = 1$.

By \eqref{eq: sets}, the angle $X^{-\sigma}$ of the sector of $\mathcal{A}$ is the fraction $2/(\pi X^{\sigma})$ of the angle $\pi/2$ of $\mathcal{B}$.

\begin{definition}[Sums and asymptotic formulas]
\label{def: asymptotic formula}
A \emph{sum} is a weighted count $\Sigma(\mathcal{E})$ of the elements of $\mathcal{E} \in \{\mathcal{A}, \mathcal{B}\}$, in which each element is counted with a real weight that does not depend on $\mathcal{E}$. A sum \emph{has an asymptotic formula} if, for every $A > 0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: asymptotic formula}
\Sigma(\mathcal{A}) = \frac{2}{\pi X^{\sigma}}\, \Sigma(\mathcal{B}) + \mathcal{O}\bigl( X^{\tau} (\log X)^{-A} \bigr),
\end{equation}
where the implied constant may depend on $A$ and on the parameters allowed by the convention on implied constants below. The sums of a family, indexed by parameters, have asymptotic formulas \emph{uniformly} in these parameters when \eqref{eq: asymptotic formula} holds with the same implied constant for every member.
\end{definition}

For example, by \eqref{eq: multiples} and \eqref{eq: cofactor sieve}, $|\mathcal{E}_u|$ gives the weight $1$ to the multiples of $u$ and $0$ to the other elements, and $S'(\mathcal{E}_u, z)$ gives the weight $1$ to the multiples of $u$ whose cofactor has no prime factor of norm below $z$. A linear combination of sums is again a sum. If the members of a family have asymptotic formulas uniformly, then, for a fixed $C$, a sum of at most $(\log X)^C$ members, each with the sign $+$ or $-$, has an asymptotic formula, since $A$ is arbitrary.

Throughout, $\varepsilon > 0$ denotes a fixed small margin. An inequality between exponents holds \emph{with margin} $\varepsilon$ if it still holds after $\varepsilon$ is added to its smaller side. Implied constants depend only on $A$, on $\theta$, on $\varepsilon$, on the exponent $C_0$ of the divisor bounds, on the numbers of variables and of constraints, and on the constants $C_1$, $C_l$ and $\varepsilon_0$ of Lemmas~\ref{lem: type II}, \ref{lem: fundamental}, \ref{lem: level large}, \ref{lem: level small} and~\ref{lem: pieces}. They never depend on dyadic ranges, intervals, levels, coefficients or the bounds $Z_c$ and $Z_{\mathrm{M}}$ of Definition~\ref{def: configuration}. This convention extends that of \citet[p.~121]{harman2001gaussian}. The letter $C$ denotes a positive constant with the same dependence, not necessarily the same at each occurrence.

The function $z(y)$ of \eqref{eq: z function} bounds the levels that Lemmas~\ref{lem: level large} and~\ref{lem: level small} admit: a sum $\sum_n a_n S'(\mathcal{E}_n, l(n))$ over $|n|^2 \Le X^{\tau - \varepsilon}$, with $a_n \Ge 0$ divisor-bounded, has an asymptotic formula when the level $l(n)$ is at most a constant times $z(|n|^2)$. For real $y \Ge 1$, define the integer $j(y)$ by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: j function}
j(y) := \begin{cases} -1 & \text{if } y \Ge X^{\sigma}, \\ \bigl\lceil \log(X^{\sigma}/y)/(\theta \log X) \bigr\rceil - 1 & \text{if } y < X^{\sigma}. \end{cases}
\end{equation}
For $y < X^{\sigma}$, \eqref{eq: j function} gives $j(y) \Ge 0$ and
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: j interval}
X^{\sigma - (j(y)+1)\theta} \Le y < X^{\sigma - j(y)\theta} \qquad (y < X^{\sigma}).
\end{equation}
Following \citet[p.~128]{harman2001gaussian}, put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: z function}
z(y) := \Bigl( \frac{X^{\tau}}{y} \Bigr)^{1/(j(y) + 2)}.
\end{equation}
By \eqref{eq: j function} and \eqref{eq: z function}, $z(y) = X^{\tau}/y$ for $y \Ge X^{\sigma}$. For $y < X^{\sigma}$, \eqref{eq: z function}, the bound $y < X^{\sigma - j(y)\theta}$ of \eqref{eq: j interval} and the relation $\tau = \sigma + 2\theta$ of \eqref{eq: parameters} give
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: z lower}
z(y) > \Bigl( \frac{X^{\tau}}{X^{\sigma - j(y)\theta}} \Bigr)^{1/(j(y)+2)} = X^{\theta} \qquad (y < X^{\sigma}).
\end{equation}

Table~\ref{tab: notation} lists the main notation of the paper.

\section{The decomposition and the total loss}
\label{sec: outline}

By \eqref{eq: sets} and Definition~\ref{def: sifting}, the number of Gaussian primes in $\mathcal{A}$ is the sifting function $S(\mathcal{A}, X^{1/2})$. Lemma~\ref{lem: lower bound} proves the lower bound \eqref{eq: lower bound intro} in the form \eqref{eq: lower bound}, in which $\Phi(\theta)$ is replaced by a number $\Phi_\varepsilon(\theta)$ that tends to the total loss $\Phi(\theta)$ of \eqref{eq: total loss} as $\varepsilon \to 0$.

\subsection{The linear and bilinear estimates in exponent form}
\label{subsec: exponent form}

The asymptotic formulas rest on the linear and bilinear estimates, Lemmas~\ref{lem: type I} and~\ref{lem: type II}. The arithmetic information quoted from \citet{harman2001gaussian} enters the argument only through these two lemmas. Consider a sum over primes $\varpi_1, \dots, \varpi_K$ and a cofactor $t$ with $\varpi_1 \cdots \varpi_K t \in \mathcal{E}$, where $|\varpi_i|^2$ is about $X^{x_i}$. For a set $\mathcal{S}$ of indices, put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: index sums}
x_{\mathcal{S}} := \sum_{i \in \mathcal{S}} x_i.
\end{equation} The linear estimate applies when $t$ is \emph{free}, that is, subject to no sieve condition. Through Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental}, it also applies when $t$ has no prime factor of norm below $\mu^4$. In exponent form, it needs $\sum_{i=1}^{K} x_i \Le \tau$, or, for a partition of the indices into sets $\mathcal{U}$ and $\mathcal{V}$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: linear exponents}
x_{\mathcal{U}} \Le \zeta, \qquad x_{\mathcal{V}} \Le \rho, \qquad \sum_{i=1}^{K} x_i \Le \nu.
\end{equation}
The bilinear estimate applies at every level of sifting of $t$, since $t$ becomes part of one of its variables, whose coefficients may be any non-negative divisor-bounded sequence. It needs an index $i_0$ and a non-empty set $\mathcal{S}$ of other indices with
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: bilinear exponents}
\sigma \Le x_{\mathcal{S}} \Le \tau - x_{i_0}.
\end{equation}
The product of the primes of $\mathcal{S}$ then has norm at least $X^{\sigma}$, up to a constant factor. So does the quotient of $\varpi_1 \cdots \varpi_K t$ by this product and by $\varpi_{i_0}$, since \eqref{eq: sets}, \eqref{eq: bilinear exponents} and \eqref{eq: parameters} give, up to a constant factor,
\begin{equation*}
\frac{|\varpi_1 \cdots \varpi_K t|^2}{|\varpi_{i_0} \prod_{i \in \mathcal{S}} \varpi_i|^2} \Ge \frac{X/2}{X^{x_{\mathcal{S}} + x_{i_0}}} \Ge \frac{X^{1 - \tau}}{2} = \frac{X^{\sigma}}{2}.
\end{equation*} The product and the quotient are the variables $u$ and $v$ of Lemma~\ref{lem: type II}, and $\varpi_{i_0}$ is its prime variable $\varpi$. Lemma~\ref{lem: pieces} states the condition $\sum_{i=1}^{K} x_i \Le \tau$ and \eqref{eq: linear exponents}, with margin $\varepsilon$, as (P1) for a free cofactor and as (P2) for a cofactor without prime factors of norm below $\mu^4$. It states \eqref{eq: bilinear exponents}, up to constant factors in the norms, as (P3). Through Lemmas~\ref{lem: level large} and~\ref{lem: level small}, its condition (P4) covers a cofactor sifted at a constant level that is at most a constant times $z(|\varpi_1 \cdots \varpi_K|^2)$, for products of norm at most $X^{\tau - \varepsilon}$.

\subsection{Decomposition of the sifting function}
\label{subsec: decomposition}

The decomposition begins with two applications of Buchstab's identity \eqref{eq: buchstab}. Put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: Pi}
\Pi_1 := \bigl\{ p : X^{\theta} \Le |p|^2 < X^{1/2},\ z(|p|^2) \Le |p|^2 \bigr\}, \qquad \Pi_2 := \bigl\{ p : X^{\theta} \Le |p|^2 < X^{1/2},\ z(|p|^2) > |p|^2 \bigr\}.
\end{equation}
For $\mathcal{E} \in \{\mathcal{A}, \mathcal{B}\}$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: identity 37}
S(\mathcal{E}, X^{1/2}) = S(\mathcal{E}, X^{\theta}) - \sum_{p \in \Pi_1} S\bigl(\mathcal{E}_p, z(|p|^2)\bigr) - \sum_{p \in \Pi_2} S(\mathcal{E}_p, p) + \sideset{}{^*}\sum_{p, q} S(\mathcal{E}_{pq}, q),
\end{equation}
where $\sum^*$ runs over the pairs with $p \in \Pi_1$, $q \prec p$ and $|q|^2 \Ge z(|p|^2)$. The first application of Buchstab's identity gives the terms $S(\mathcal{E}_p, p)$ with $X^{\theta} \Le |p|^2 < X^{1/2}$. Since $|p|^2 < z(|p|^2)$ for $p \in \Pi_2$ by \eqref{eq: Pi}, the sum over $p \in \Pi_2$ in \eqref{eq: identity 37} has an asymptotic formula without a second application. For $p \in \Pi_1$, the second application lowers the level of $S(\mathcal{E}_p, p)$ to $z(|p|^2)$ and subtracts the terms of $\sum^*$. Lemma~\ref{lem: identity 37} proves \eqref{eq: identity 37} and gives the terms other than $\sum^*$ asymptotic formulas by Corollary~\ref{cor: level one} and Lemmas~\ref{lem: type I}, \ref{lem: level large} and~\ref{lem: level small}. The rest of the argument concerns $\sum^*$.

The lemmas of Section~\ref{sec: two primes} treat a pair $(p, q)$ of $\sum^*$ according to the sizes of $|p|^2$ and $|q|^2$ as powers of $X$. For primes $p, q, r, s$, define the exponents $\alpha, \beta, \gamma, \delta$ by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: exponents}
|p|^2 = X^{\alpha}, \qquad |q|^2 = X^{\beta}, \qquad |r|^2 = X^{\gamma}, \qquad |s|^2 = X^{\delta}.
\end{equation}
For $0 < \alpha < 1/2$, the lower limit of $\beta$ in $\sum^*$ is, by \eqref{eq: j function} and \eqref{eq: z function},
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: beta0}
\beta_0(\alpha) := \frac{\log z(X^{\alpha})}{\log X} = \begin{cases} \tau - \alpha & \text{for } \alpha \Ge \sigma, \\ (\tau - \alpha)/(j(X^{\alpha}) + 2) & \text{for } \alpha < \sigma. \end{cases}
\end{equation}
Section~\ref{sec: decomposition} discards the pairs of $\sum^*$ with $|p|^2|q|^4 \Ge X$, whose terms have cofactor $1$ and whose sum over $\mathcal{B}$ is $\mathcal{O}(X/(\log X)^2)$. The other pairs with $|q| < |p|$ have exponents \eqref{eq: exponents} in
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: domain}
\mathsf{D} := \bigl\{ (\alpha, \beta) : \theta \Le \alpha < 1/2,\ \beta_0(\alpha) \Le \beta < \min(\alpha, (1 - \alpha)/2) \bigr\}.
\end{equation}

The other pairs with $|q| = |p|$ have exponents on the line $\beta = \alpha$, on the boundary of $\mathsf{D}$. Following \citet[\S5]{harman2001gaussian}, whose numbering we keep, we split $\mathsf{D}$ into Ranges~1 to~6, listed in Table~\ref{tab: ranges}, and each of them into parts, which are polytopes. Figure~\ref{fig: domain} shows the ranges at $\theta = 0.1129$, with the regions $\mathsf{D}_k$ of Section~\ref{sec: decomposition} where $\sum^*$ is discarded and the region $\mathsf{R}$ of \eqref{eq: region R}. On each part, $\sum^*$ receives one of three \emph{treatments}: an asymptotic formula for the whole sum; identity \eqref{eq: identity 35} of Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 13} or identity \eqref{eq: identity 36} of Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 14}; or a discard of the whole sum. Both identities apply Buchstab's identity \eqref{eq: buchstab} twice. For $|pq|^2 \Ge X^{\sigma}$, the level $X^{\tau}/|pq|^2$ is the largest, up to a constant factor, that Lemma~\ref{lem: level large} admits for a sum of $S(\mathcal{A}_{pq}, \cdot)$ over $(p, q)$. The conditions $|pq|^2 \Le X^{\tau} \Le |p|^2|q|^4$ make this level at least $1$ and at most $|q|^2$. For a pair $(p, q)$ with $q \prec p$ that satisfies them, the two applications of Buchstab's identity \eqref{eq: buchstab} at the level $X^{\tau}/|pq|^2$ give
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: pair identity}
S(\mathcal{A}_{pq}, q) = S\Bigl(\mathcal{A}_{pq}, \frac{X^{\tau}}{|pq|^2}\Bigr) - \sum_{\substack{r \prec q \\ |pqr|^2 \Ge X^{\tau}}} S\Bigl(\mathcal{A}_{pqr}, \frac{X^{\tau}}{|pq|^2}\Bigr) + \sum_{\substack{s \prec r \prec q \\ |pqs|^2 \Ge X^{\tau}}} S(\mathcal{A}_{pqrs}, s).
\end{equation}
In each term $S(\mathcal{A}_u, \cdot)$ of \eqref{eq: pair identity}, every prime factor of $u$ satisfies the condition that the level imposes, by $s \prec r \prec q \prec p$, the bound $|p|^2|q|^4 \Ge X^{\tau}$ and the conditions of summation. So these terms equal $S'(\mathcal{A}_u, \cdot)$ by \eqref{eq: sifting} and \eqref{eq: cofactor sieve}, as \eqref{eq: buchstab} requires. In the third sum, $s \prec r$ and $|pqs|^2 \Ge X^{\tau}$ give $|pqr|^2 \Ge X^{\tau}$. A \emph{box} is the set of pairs $(p, q)$ with $p \sim P$ and $q \sim Q$, for powers $P$ and $Q$ of $2$. Its \emph{exponent rectangle} is the set of pairs $(\alpha, \beta)$ with $X^{\alpha} \in [P, 2P)$ and $X^{\beta} \in [Q, 2Q)$, and the distance of a box from a set of exponents is that of its exponent rectangle. Summing \eqref{eq: pair identity}, with $PQ$ in place of $|pq|^2$, over the pairs $(p, q)$ of a box with $X^{\sigma} \Le PQ \Le X^{\tau}$ that satisfy $q \prec p$ and $|q|^2 \Ge X^{\tau}/(PQ)$ gives identity \eqref{eq: identity 35}. For the boxes with $PQ < X^{\sigma}$, \eqref{eq: j function} gives $j(PQ) \Ge 0$, so the level $X^{\tau}/(PQ)$ exceeds $z(PQ)$ by \eqref{eq: z function}. Identity \eqref{eq: identity 36} is built in the same way, at the level $z(PQ)$ and at a level that depends on $|r|^2$. On the parts whose treatment is an identity, the sums over $(p, q)$ and over $(p, q, r)$ of that identity have asymptotic formulas. The \emph{third sum} of identity \eqref{eq: identity 35} or \eqref{eq: identity 36}, of $S(\mathcal{A}_{pqrs}, s)$ over primes $s \prec r \prec q \prec p$, has non-negative terms and enters the identity with the sign $+$. It is discarded over a four-dimensional region of exponents $(\alpha, \beta, \gamma, \delta)$.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Groupings on the regions $\mathsf{R}$, $\mathsf{G}$ and $\mathsf{L}$}{Groupings on the regions R, G and L}}
\label{subsec: groupings}

The grouping in Range~2 of Table~\ref{tab: ranges} acts on the region
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: region R}
\mathsf{R} := \bigl\{ 1/3 \Le \alpha < \sigma,\ \max(\zeta - \alpha, (\tau - \alpha)/2) \Le \beta \Le \rho/2 \bigr\}.
\end{equation}
Case (a) of \eqref{eq: lemma 12 cases} in Lemma~\ref{lem: three primes}, whose conditions read $\alpha + \beta \Le \zeta$, $\beta \Le \rho$ and $\alpha + 2\beta \Le \nu$ in exponents, applies the linear estimate to the factorization $(pq) \cdot r$. In Range~2, $\alpha + \beta \Le \zeta$ implies $\beta \Le \rho$ and $\alpha + 2\beta \Le \nu$ (Section~\ref{sec: decomposition}). On $\mathsf{R}$, where $\alpha + \beta \Ge \zeta$, case (b), whose conditions read $\alpha \Le \zeta$, $2\beta \Le \rho$ and $\alpha + 2\beta \Le \nu$ in exponents, applies the linear estimate to the factorization $p \cdot (qr)$. In Range~2, $\alpha < \sigma < \zeta$ by Table~\ref{tab: inequalities} and $\sigma + \rho = \nu$ by \eqref{eq: parameters}, so these conditions reduce to $2\beta \Le \rho$, the upper limit of $\beta$ in \eqref{eq: region R}. The lower limits of $\beta$ there are $\zeta - \alpha$, below which case (a) applies, and $\beta_0(\alpha)$. In Range~2, $\sigma - \theta < 1/3 \Le \alpha < \sigma$ by Table~\ref{tab: inequalities}, so $X^{\sigma - \theta} < X^{\alpha} < X^{\sigma}$ and \eqref{eq: j interval} gives $j(X^{\alpha}) = 0$. Hence $\beta_0(\alpha) = (\tau - \alpha)/2$ by \eqref{eq: beta0}. Section~\ref{sec: decomposition} checks that $X^{\sigma} \Le PQ \Le X^{\tau - \varepsilon}$ on $\mathsf{R}$, as Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 13} requires. So on $\mathsf{R}$, the treatment of $\sum^*$ is identity \eqref{eq: identity 35}, whose third sum alone is discarded, over the region $\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{R}}$ of \eqref{eq: four dim regions}.

The other two groupings give parts of the third sums of \eqref{eq: identity 35} and \eqref{eq: identity 36} asymptotic formulas. The exponents \eqref{eq: exponents} of $p, q, r, s$ are the $x_i$ of \eqref{eq: index sums}. An index set is denoted by the set of its primes, as in $x_{\{p, q\}} = \alpha + \beta$, and $i_0$ is given by its prime, as in $\varpi_{i_0} = s$. On a region $\mathsf{G}$, the bilinear estimate applies to the products $pqrst$ with $s$ as its prime variable: \eqref{eq: bilinear exponents} holds with $x_{i_0} = \delta$ for a non-empty set $\mathcal{S} \subset \{p, q, r\}$. On a region $\mathsf{L}$, Buchstab's identity \eqref{eq: buchstab} at the level $\mu^4$ writes $S(\mathcal{A}_{pqrs}, s)$ as the difference \eqref{eq: L split} of a sum sifted at the level $\mu^4$ and a sum over a further prime $s' \prec s$. The sum over $s'$ enters with the sign $-$, so both sums need asymptotic formulas. The linear estimate treats the sum sifted at the level $\mu^4$ on the region $\mathsf{I}$ where the exponents of $p, q, r, s$ satisfy $\alpha + \beta + \gamma + \delta \Le \tau$ or \eqref{eq: linear exponents}. Since $|s'|^2 \Le |s|^2$, the bilinear estimate with $s'$ as its prime variable treats the sum over $s'$ where \eqref{eq: bilinear exponents} holds with $\delta$ in place of $x_{i_0}$ for a non-empty set $\mathcal{S} \subset \{p, q, r, s\}$. The regions $\mathsf{G}$, $\mathsf{I}$ and $\mathsf{L}$ are
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: regions G L}
\begin{aligned}
\mathsf{G} &:= \bigcup_{\emptyset \neq \mathcal{S} \subset \{p, q, r\}} \bigl\{ \sigma \Le x_{\mathcal{S}} \Le \tau - \delta \bigr\}, \\
\mathsf{I} &:= \bigl\{ \alpha + \beta + \gamma + \delta \Le \tau \bigr\} \cup \bigcup_{\mathcal{U} \sqcup \mathcal{V} = \{p, q, r, s\}} \bigl\{ x_{\mathcal{U}} \Le \zeta,\ x_{\mathcal{V}} \Le \rho,\ \alpha + \beta + \gamma + \delta \Le \nu \bigr\}, \\
\mathsf{L} &:= \mathsf{I} \cap \bigcup_{\emptyset \neq \mathcal{S} \subset \{p, q, r, s\}} \bigl\{ \sigma \Le x_{\mathcal{S}} \Le \tau - \delta \bigr\}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
Lemma~\ref{lem: four dim} gives the third sums asymptotic formulas on $\mathsf{G}$ and $\mathsf{L}$. These regions use the smallest prime of the product $pqrs$ or $pqrss'$, namely $s$ or $s'$, as the prime variable of the bilinear estimate. For a product with fixed exponents, such as $pqrs$ on $\mathsf{G}$, another prime in this role would not enlarge the region. Indeed, let $\varpi_{i_1}$ be the smallest prime of the product and $\varpi_{i_0}$ another prime of it, and suppose that \eqref{eq: bilinear exponents} holds for a set $\mathcal{S}$ of primes of the product with $\varpi_{i_0} \notin \mathcal{S}$. If $\varpi_{i_1} \notin \mathcal{S}$, then \eqref{eq: bilinear exponents} holds with $i_1$ in place of $i_0$, since $x_{i_1} \Le x_{i_0}$. If $\varpi_{i_1} \in \mathcal{S}$, replacing it in $\mathcal{S}$ by $\varpi_{i_0}$ raises $x_{\mathcal{S}}$ by $x_{i_0} - x_{i_1}$. By \eqref{eq: bilinear exponents} for $\mathcal{S}$, the new value $x_{\mathcal{S}} + x_{i_0} - x_{i_1}$ satisfies
\begin{equation*}
\sigma \Le x_{\mathcal{S}} \Le x_{\mathcal{S}} + x_{i_0} - x_{i_1} \Le \tau - x_{i_1}.
\end{equation*}
So the new set satisfies \eqref{eq: bilinear exponents} with $i_1$ in place of $i_0$.

The region $\mathsf{L}$ contains the polytope
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: polytope}
\mathsf{P} := \bigl\{ \alpha + \beta \Le \sigma,\ \gamma + \delta \Le \rho,\ \alpha + \beta + \delta \Ge \sigma,\ \alpha + \beta + 2\delta \Le \tau \bigr\}.
\end{equation}
On $\mathsf{P}$, the partition $\mathcal{U} = \{p, q\}$, $\mathcal{V} = \{r, s\}$ satisfies \eqref{eq: linear exponents}, since Table~\ref{tab: inequalities} and \eqref{eq: parameters} give
\begin{equation*}
x_{\mathcal{U}} = \alpha + \beta \Le \sigma < \zeta, \qquad x_{\mathcal{V}} = \gamma + \delta \Le \rho, \qquad \alpha + \beta + \gamma + \delta \Le \sigma + \rho = \nu.
\end{equation*} The set $\mathcal{S} = \{p, q, s\}$ has $x_{\mathcal{S}} = \alpha + \beta + \delta$, so the bounds $\alpha + \beta + \delta \Ge \sigma$ and $\alpha + \beta + 2\delta \Le \tau$ of \eqref{eq: polytope} are \eqref{eq: bilinear exponents} with $\delta$ in place of $x_{i_0}$. Section~\ref{sec: numerics} also evaluates the losses with $\mathsf{P}$ in place of $\mathsf{L}$. On $\mathsf{P}$, the treatment of each four-dimensional box of Section~\ref{sec: four} can be checked by hand.

\subsection{The total loss}
\label{subsec: total loss}

The lower bound of Lemma~\ref{lem: lower bound} drops the discarded sums over $\mathcal{A}$, and its main term is $2/(\pi X^{\sigma})$ times the difference between $S(\mathcal{B}, X^{1/2})$ and the same sums over $\mathcal{B}$, as in \eqref{eq: lower bound boxes}. So each discarded sum is measured relative to $S(\mathcal{B}, X^{1/2})$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: boxes to integrals}, a discarded sum over $\mathcal{B}$ with exponents in a region, divided by $S(\mathcal{B}, X^{1/2})$, tends to the loss of that region (Definition~\ref{def: loss}).

\begin{definition}[Losses]
\label{def: loss}
The \emph{losses} of a two-dimensional region $\mathsf{F}$ and of a four-dimensional region $\mathsf{F}'$ are
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: loss}
\Phi(\mathsf{F}) := \iint_{\mathsf{F}} \omega\Bigl( \frac{1 - \alpha - \beta}{\beta} \Bigr) \frac{d\beta\, d\alpha}{\alpha \beta^2}, \qquad \Phi(\mathsf{F}') := \iiiint_{\mathsf{F}'} \omega\Bigl( \frac{1 - \alpha - \beta - \gamma - \delta}{\delta} \Bigr) \frac{d\delta\, d\gamma\, d\beta\, d\alpha}{\alpha \beta \gamma \delta^2},
\end{equation}
where $\omega$ is \emph{Buchstab's function}, defined by $\omega(u) = 0$ for $u < 1$ and
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: buchstab function}
u\omega(u) = 1 + \int_1^{u-1} \omega(t)\, dt \qquad (u \Ge 1).
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

Equation \eqref{eq: buchstab function} defines $\omega$ implicitly, starting from $\omega(u) = 1/u$ for $1 \Le u \Le 2$, where the integral vanishes.

\begin{definition}[Total loss]
\label{def: total loss}
The \emph{total loss} is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: total loss}
\Phi(\theta) := \sum_{k \in \{1, 2, 4, 6, 8\}} \Phi(\mathsf{D}_k) + \sum_{k \in \{3, \mathsf{R}, 5, 7, 9, 10, 11\}} \Phi\bigl(\mathsf{E}_k \setminus (\mathsf{G} \cup \mathsf{L})\bigr),
\end{equation}
with $\mathsf{D}_k$ of \eqref{eq: D1}, \eqref{eq: D2}, \eqref{eq: D4}, \eqref{eq: D6} and \eqref{eq: D8}, $\mathsf{E}_k$ of \eqref{eq: four dim regions}, and $\mathsf{G}$, $\mathsf{L}$ of \eqref{eq: regions G L}. Let $\Phi_{\mathsf{P}}(\theta)$ be defined in the same way with $\mathsf{P}$ of \eqref{eq: polytope} in place of $\mathsf{L}$.
\end{definition}

The letter $\Phi$ denotes the loss \eqref{eq: loss} when its argument is a region, and the total loss when its argument is $\theta$.

The total loss collects the losses of the regions of all sums that remain discarded: the sums over $(p, q)$ on the regions $\mathsf{D}_k$, and the third sums on the regions $\mathsf{E}_k$ outside $\mathsf{G} \cup \mathsf{L}$, since Lemma~\ref{lem: four dim} gives the third sums asymptotic formulas on $\mathsf{G} \cup \mathsf{L}$. So $\Phi(\theta)$ measures the proportion of $S(\mathcal{B}, X^{1/2})$ that the discarded sums take up, and the lower bound of Lemma~\ref{lem: lower bound} is positive when $\Phi(\theta) < 1$.

The proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: lower bound} splits $\sum^*$ and the third sums into dyadic boxes. It applies Lemmas~\ref{lem: lemma 11}, \ref{lem: lemma 13} and~\ref{lem: lemma 14} to the boxes that lie inside a part where these lemmas apply, at distance at least $\varepsilon$ from its boundary, and Lemma~\ref{lem: four dim} to the four-dimensional boxes that lie in $\mathsf{G}$ or $\mathsf{L}$ with margin $\varepsilon$. It bounds the sums over the other boxes by Lemma~\ref{lem: boxes to integrals}.

\section{Asymptotic formulas from the linear and bilinear estimates}
\label{sec: estimates}

Each result of this section gives a sum an asymptotic formula in the sense of Definition~\ref{def: asymptotic formula}. Lemmas~\ref{lem: type I} and~\ref{lem: type II} below are the linear and bilinear estimates of Section~\ref{sec: outline}, in the form used in this paper. The other results of this section treat sums in which the cofactor is sifted. Lemmas~\ref{lem: level large} and~\ref{lem: level small} sift the cofactors of the elements of $\mathcal{E}_n$ at a level $l(n)$ that depends on $n$.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem: type I}
Let $c_v \Ge 0$ be divisor-bounded, and suppose that one of the following holds:
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
\item $c_v$ is supported on $|v|^2 \Le X^{\tau} \mu^{-1}$;
\item $c_v = \sum_{v = uu'} a_u b_{u'}$, where $a_u, b_{u'} \Ge 0$ are divisor-bounded and supported on $u \sim U$, $u' \sim U'$, with $U \Le X^{\zeta} \mu^{-1}$, $U' \Le X^{\rho} \mu^{-1}$ and $UU' \Le X^{\nu} \mu^{-1}$.
\end{enumerate}
Then $\sum_{v \neq 0} c_v |\mathcal{E}_v|$ has an asymptotic formula.
\emph{(Proof in Appendix~\ref{app: arithmetic}.)}
\end{lemma}

The bilinear estimate of \citet[Lemma~5 and (30)]{harman2001gaussian} needs a prime variable. Its proof applies when the prime is restricted to any interval of norms contained in $[\mu^4/2, \infty)$, as the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: type II} in Appendix~\ref{app: arithmetic} shows. Remark~\ref{rem: lemma 4 range} explains the lower limit $\mu^4/2$, and Section~\ref{sec: decoupling} restricts the prime to short intervals.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem: type II}
Let $C_1 \Ge 1$, let $a_u, b_v \Ge 0$ be divisor-bounded and supported on $u \sim U$ and $v \sim V$, and let $\mu^4/2 \Le Z_1 < Z_2$. Suppose that $U, V \Ge X^{\sigma}/C_1$. Then
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: type II}
\sum_{u \sim U,\ v \sim V} a_u b_v\, \#\{ \varpi : Z_1 \Le |\varpi|^2 < Z_2,\ uv\varpi \in \mathcal{E} \}
\end{equation}
has an asymptotic formula, uniformly in $U$, $V$, $Z_1$ and $Z_2$.
\emph{(Proof in Appendix~\ref{app: arithmetic}.)}
\end{lemma}

Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental} below is a fundamental lemma over $\mathbb{Z}[i]$. Every level at which it is applied is at most $\mu^4 = X^{o(1)}$, so Brun's pure sieve, described in Appendix~\ref{app: sieve}, suffices.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem: fundamental}
Let $0 < \varepsilon_0 < 1$, let $c_u \Ge 0$ be divisor-bounded, and let $z_0$ be a function on the support of $(c_u)$ with $1 \Le z_0(u) \Le \mu^4$. Then $\sum_{u \neq 0} c_u S'(\mathcal{E}_u, z_0(u))$ has an asymptotic formula if one of the following holds:
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
\item $c_u$ is supported on $|u|^2 \Le X^{\tau - \varepsilon_0} \mu^{-1}$;
\item $z_0$ is constant, and $c_u = \sum_{u = u_1 u_2} a_{u_1} b_{u_2}$, where $a_{u_1}, b_{u_2} \Ge 0$ are divisor-bounded and supported on $u_1 \sim U_1$, $u_2 \sim U_2$, with $U_1 \Le X^{\zeta} \mu^{-1}$, $U_2 \Le X^{\rho - \varepsilon_0} \mu^{-1}$ and $U_1U_2 \Le X^{\nu - \varepsilon_0} \mu^{-1}$.
\end{enumerate}
\emph{(Proof in Appendix~\ref{app: sieve}.)}
\end{lemma}

Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental} sifts at levels up to $\mu^4$, and the decomposition needs levels $l(n)$ up to a constant times $X^{\tau}/|n|^2$ for $|n|^2 \Ge X^{\sigma}$ and up to a constant times $z(|n|^2)$ for $|n|^2 < X^{\sigma}$. Lemmas~\ref{lem: level large} and~\ref{lem: level small} reach these levels through the bilinear estimate. Buchstab's identity \eqref{eq: buchstab} lowers the level $l(n)$ to $\mu^4$, where Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental} applies, and subtracts a sum over a prime $q$ with $\mu^4 \Le |q|^2 < l(n)$. Once $q$ is confined to short intervals, $n$ is the variable $u$ of Lemma~\ref{lem: type II} in this sum, and $q$ is its prime variable. If $|n|^2 \Ge X^{\sigma}/C_l$ and $l(n) \Le C_l X^{\tau}/|n|^2$ for a constant $C_l \Ge 1$, then \eqref{eq: sets} and \eqref{eq: parameters} give, for the cofactor $v$ of an element of $\mathcal{E}_{nq}$,
\begin{equation*}
|v|^2 > \frac{X/2}{|n|^2\, l(n)} \Ge \frac{X^{1 - \tau}}{2C_l} = \frac{X^{\sigma}}{2C_l},
\end{equation*}
and Lemma~\ref{lem: type II} applies. The bounds on $|n|^2$ and $l(n)$ are among the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem: level large}.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem: level large}
Let $C_l \Ge 1$, let $a_n \Ge 0$ be divisor-bounded and supported on $X^{\sigma}/C_l \Le |n|^2 \Le X^{\tau - \varepsilon}$, and let $l$ be any function on the support of $(a_n)$ with $1 \Le l(n) \Le C_l X^{\tau}/|n|^2$. Then $\sum_{X^{\sigma}/C_l \Le |n|^2 \Le X^{\tau - \varepsilon}} a_n S'(\mathcal{E}_n, l(n))$ has an asymptotic formula, uniformly in $(a_n)$ and $l$.
\emph{(Proof in Appendix~\ref{app: sieve}.)}
\end{lemma}

For $|n|^2 < X^{\sigma}$, $n$ can be too small to be the variable $u$ of Lemma~\ref{lem: type II}, which needs $U \Ge X^{\sigma}/C_1$. The proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: level small} applies Buchstab's identity repeatedly, until the product of $n$ and the primes it introduces has norm at least $X^{\sigma}$, and then applies Lemma~\ref{lem: level large} to that product. The bound $l(n) \Le C_l\, z(|n|^2)$ ensures that each such product and its level satisfy the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem: level large}.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem: level small}
Let $C_l \Ge 1$, let $a_n \Ge 0$ be divisor-bounded and supported on $1 \Le |n|^2 < X^{\sigma}$, and let $l$ be any function on the support of $(a_n)$ with $1 \Le l(n) \Le C_l\, z(|n|^2)$, where $z(|n|^2)$ is given by \eqref{eq: z function}. Then $\sum_{1 \Le |n|^2 < X^{\sigma}} a_n S'(\mathcal{E}_n, l(n))$ has an asymptotic formula, uniformly in $(a_n)$ and $l$.
\emph{(Proof in Appendix~\ref{app: sieve}.)}
\end{lemma}

\begin{corollary}
\label{cor: level one}
The sum $S(\mathcal{E}, X^{\theta})$ of \eqref{eq: identity 37} has an asymptotic formula.
\end{corollary}

\begin{proof}
By \eqref{eq: multiples}, \eqref{eq: sifting} and \eqref{eq: cofactor sieve}, the sum $S(\mathcal{E}, X^{\theta}) = S'(\mathcal{E}_1, X^{\theta})$ is the case $C_l = 1$, $a_n = \mathbf{1}[n = 1]$, $l \equiv X^{\theta}$ of Lemma~\ref{lem: level small}. The hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem: level small} hold, since $1 < X^{\sigma}$ and $1 \Le X^{\theta} < z(1)$ by \eqref{eq: z lower}.
\end{proof}

\section{Removing the conditions that couple the variables}
\label{sec: decoupling}

The sums of Harman's sieve run over products of primes with order constraints between them, conditions on products of their norms, and sieve conditions on the cofactor that depend on one of the primes. The bilinear estimate of Lemma~\ref{lem: type II} admits none of these conditions between its variables. This section removes them with short intervals. The order constraints and the conditions on products of norms are the cross-conditions of \citet[\S7]{merikoski2023polynomials}, and the short intervals give what he calls a finer-than-dyadic decomposition, which he builds from smooth weights \citep[\S3.1]{merikoski2023polynomials}. \citet[\S11.1]{heathbrown2017prime} separate variables by short intervals of the same shape as ours, and \citet[p.~130]{harman2001gaussian} remove such conditions by Perron's formula (Appendix~\ref{app: comparison}). Every order constraint and every condition on a product of norms is constant on most Cartesian products of short intervals, and the sums over the other products are small. On a single Cartesian product, the sieve condition differs from sifting at a constant level only at the prime factors of the cofactor whose norms lie in a few short intervals. Lemma~\ref{lem: decoupling} reduces a sum with such conditions to sums over single Cartesian products of short intervals, and Lemma~\ref{lem: pieces} gives these sums asymptotic formulas under the conditions of Section~\ref{subsec: exponent form}.

For a fixed $B > 0$, put, for integers $\lambda \Ge 0$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: short intervals}
H := (\log X)^B, \qquad \xi_\lambda := (1 + 1/H)^{\lambda}, \qquad J_\lambda := [\xi_\lambda, \xi_{\lambda + 1}).
\end{equation}
The intervals $J_\lambda$ are the \emph{short intervals}. For a prime $q$, let $\lambda_q$ be the index with $|q|^2 \in J_{\lambda_q}$. The proofs of Lemmas~\ref{lem: level large}, \ref{lem: level small} and~\ref{lem: decoupling} in Appendix~\ref{app: sieve} choose $B$ in terms of $A$.

\begin{definition}[Configurations]
\label{def: configuration}
A \emph{configuration} $\mathcal{C}$ consists of the following data:
\begin{enumerate}[(i)]
\item prime variables $\varpi_1, \dots, \varpi_k$, with $k$ fixed and $|\varpi_i|^2 \in \mathcal{I}_i$ for intervals $\mathcal{I}_i \subset [\mu^4, X]$, the \emph{ranges} of the variables. A \emph{tuple} is a choice $\boldsymbol{\varpi} = (\varpi_1, \dots, \varpi_k)$ of these primes;
\item a cofactor $t$, which is a Gaussian integer;
\item a fixed number of \emph{monomial constraints} $c$, each of the form $m_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi}) < Z_c$ or $m_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi}) \Le Z_c$, where $m_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi}) := \prod_{i=1}^{k} |\varpi_i|^{2e_{c,i}}$ with integers $|e_{c,i}| \Le 2$, not all $0$, and $Z_c > 0$. We write $\mathbf{1}_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi})$ for the indicator that $\boldsymbol{\varpi}$ satisfies $c$. A lower bound $m_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi}) \Ge Z_c$ is the constraint $m_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi})^{-1} \Le Z_c^{-1}$. An \emph{order constraint} $\varpi_i \prec \varpi_{i'}$ is the monomial constraint with $m_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi}) = |\varpi_i|^2/|\varpi_{i'}|^2$ and $Z_c = 1$, in which the case $m_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi}) = 1$ is decided by the order \eqref{eq: order};
\item a \emph{sieve condition} on $t$, with indicator $\Xi(\boldsymbol{\varpi}, t)$, of \emph{type} (F), (C), (S) or (M): (F) none; (C) every prime factor of $t$ has norm at least $\mu^4$; (S) every prime factor $\varpi'$ of $t$ satisfies $\varpi' \succcurlyeq \varpi_{i_{\mathrm{S}}}$, for a fixed index $i_{\mathrm{S}}$; (M) every prime factor of $t$ has norm at least
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: type M level}
m_{\mathrm{M}}(\boldsymbol{\varpi}) := Z_{\mathrm{M}} \prod_{i=1}^{k} |\varpi_i|^{2f_i},
\end{equation}
with $Z_{\mathrm{M}} > 0$ and reals $|f_i| \Le 2$ that do not depend on the tuple.
\end{enumerate}
On every tuple that satisfies all the constraints, we require $m_{\mathrm{M}}(\boldsymbol{\varpi}) \Ge \mu^4$ in type (M). The \emph{sum induced by} $\mathcal{C}$ is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: configuration sum}
\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{E}) := \sum_{\varpi_1, \dots, \varpi_k} \sum_{t \in \mathbb{Z}[i]} \mathbf{1}[\varpi_1 \cdots \varpi_k t \in \mathcal{E}] \prod_{i=1}^{k} \mathbf{1}[|\varpi_i|^2 \in \mathcal{I}_i] \prod_c \mathbf{1}_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi})\, \Xi(\boldsymbol{\varpi}, t).
\end{equation}
A configuration has the type of its sieve condition, and we identify it with the sum $\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}$ that it induces.
\end{definition}

For example, for fixed primes $p, q, r, s$, the sum $S'(\mathcal{E}_{pqrs}, s)$ counts, by \eqref{eq: cofactor sieve}, the cofactors $t$ with $pqrst \in \mathcal{E}$ whose prime factors are all $\succcurlyeq s$. A sum of $S'(\mathcal{E}_{pqrs}, s)$ over primes in given ranges with $s \prec r \prec q \prec p$, such as the third sum of \eqref{eq: identity 35} on a box, is therefore a configuration with $k = 4$, the order constraints $s \prec r$, $r \prec q$ and $q \prec p$, and the sieve condition of type (S) with $i_{\mathrm{S}}$ the index of $s$.

\begin{definition}[Pieces, levels and windows]
\label{def: pieces}
Let $\mathcal{C}$ be a configuration. For an \emph{index vector} $\boldsymbol{\lambda} = (\lambda_1, \dots, \lambda_k)$ of non-negative integers, put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: tuple set}
\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) := \bigl\{ \boldsymbol{\varpi} : |\varpi_i|^2 \in \mathcal{I}_i \cap J_{\lambda_i} \text{ for } 1 \Le i \Le k \bigr\}.
\end{equation}
The \emph{pieces} of $\mathcal{C}$ are the sums over a single set $\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$, without constraints, in which the cofactor is sifted at a constant level $y \Ge 1$, or which have a further prime $\varpi'$ with norm in a short interval $J_{\lambda'}$ and a free cofactor:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: pieces}
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\mathcal{E}; y) := \sum_{\boldsymbol{\varpi} \in \mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})} S'(\mathcal{E}_{\varpi_1 \cdots \varpi_k}, y), \qquad \mathcal{P}'_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\lambda'}(\mathcal{E}) := \sum_{\boldsymbol{\varpi} \in \mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})} \sum_{|\varpi'|^2 \in J_{\lambda'}} |\mathcal{E}_{\varpi_1 \cdots \varpi_k \varpi'}|,
\end{equation}
where $\varpi'$ runs over primes and the integer $\lambda' \Ge 0$ is the index of a single short interval. The index vector $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$ \emph{satisfies} a constraint $c$ if every tuple of $\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$ satisfies $c$, and \emph{straddles} $c$ if $\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$ contains tuples that satisfy $c$ and tuples that do not. The \emph{level} of $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$ is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: piece level}
y_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}} := \begin{cases} 1 & \text{in type (F)}, \\ \mu^4 & \text{in type (C)}, \\ \xi_{\lambda_{i_{\mathrm{S}}}} & \text{in type (S)}, \\ \inf_{\boldsymbol{\varpi} \in \mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})} m_{\mathrm{M}}(\boldsymbol{\varpi}) & \text{in type (M)}, \end{cases}
\end{equation}
and its \emph{window} is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: window}
\mathcal{W}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) := \begin{cases} \emptyset & \text{in types (F) and (C)}, \\ \{\lambda_{i_{\mathrm{S}}}\} & \text{in type (S)}, \\ \{ \lambda' : J_{\lambda'} \cap [\inf_{\boldsymbol{\varpi} \in \mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})} m_{\mathrm{M}}(\boldsymbol{\varpi}), \sup_{\boldsymbol{\varpi} \in \mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})} m_{\mathrm{M}}(\boldsymbol{\varpi})) \neq \emptyset \} & \text{in type (M)}. \end{cases}
\end{equation}
\end{definition}

For $y = 1$, the cofactor of $\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\mathcal{E}; y)$ is free, since $S'(\mathcal{E}_u, 1) = |\mathcal{E}_u|$ by \eqref{eq: cofactor sieve}. By \eqref{eq: tuple set} and \eqref{eq: short intervals}, each $|\varpi_i|^2$ varies by at most the factor $1 + 1/H$ on $\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$. Since $|f_i| \Le 2$ in Definition~\ref{def: configuration}, the level $m_{\mathrm{M}}(\boldsymbol{\varpi})$ of \eqref{eq: type M level} varies there by at most the factor $(1 + 1/H)^{2k}$, so the window \eqref{eq: window} has at most $2k + 2$ elements. Suppose that $\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) \neq \emptyset$ and that $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$ satisfies all constraints. By \eqref{eq: window}, the ranges $\mathcal{I}_i \subset [\mu^4, X]$ in type (S) and the requirement $m_{\mathrm{M}}(\boldsymbol{\varpi}) \Ge \mu^4$ of Definition~\ref{def: configuration} in type (M), every $\lambda' \in \mathcal{W}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$ then has $\xi_{\lambda'+1} > \mu^4$. Hence, by \eqref{eq: short intervals},
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: window floor}
\xi_{\lambda'} \Ge \mu^4/2 \qquad (\lambda' \in \mathcal{W}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})).
\end{equation}
On the tuples of $\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$, the sieve condition $\Xi(\boldsymbol{\varpi}, t)$ and sifting at the constant level $y_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}$ of \eqref{eq: piece level} agree in types (F) and (C). In type (S) they differ only at prime factors $\varpi'$ of $t$ with $\xi_{\lambda_{i_{\mathrm{S}}}} \Le |\varpi'|^2 \Le |\varpi_{i_{\mathrm{S}}}|^2$, and in type (M) only at prime factors $\varpi'$ of $t$ with $y_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}} \Le |\varpi'|^2 < m_{\mathrm{M}}(\boldsymbol{\varpi})$. By \eqref{eq: short intervals} and \eqref{eq: window}, the norm of each such prime factor lies in $J_{\lambda'}$ for some $\lambda' \in \mathcal{W}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$. On $\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$, the indicators $\mathbf{1}[|\varpi_i|^2 \in \mathcal{I}_i]$ of \eqref{eq: configuration sum} equal $1$ by \eqref{eq: tuple set}, and the indicators $\mathbf{1}_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi})$ equal $1$ since $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$ satisfies all constraints. So, by \eqref{eq: configuration sum} and \eqref{eq: pieces}, the part of $\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}$ on the tuples of $\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$ satisfies
\begin{equation*}
\Bigl| \sum_{\boldsymbol{\varpi} \in \mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})} \sum_{t \in \mathbb{Z}[i]} \mathbf{1}[\varpi_1 \cdots \varpi_k t \in \mathcal{E}]\, \Xi(\boldsymbol{\varpi}, t) - \mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\mathcal{E}; y_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}) \Bigr| \Le \sum_{\lambda' \in \mathcal{W}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})} \mathcal{P}'_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\lambda'}(\mathcal{E}).
\end{equation*}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem: decoupling}
Let $\mathcal{C}$ be a configuration, and suppose that, for every fixed $B$, the following pieces of $\mathcal{C}$ (Definition~\ref{def: pieces}) have asymptotic formulas, uniformly in $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$ and $\lambda'$:
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
\item $\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\mathcal{E}; y_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})$, with the level $y_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}$ of \eqref{eq: piece level}, for every $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$ that satisfies all constraints;
\item $\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\mathcal{E}; 1)$ for every $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$ that straddles some constraint;
\item $\mathcal{P}'_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\lambda'}(\mathcal{E})$ for every $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$ that satisfies all constraints and every $\lambda'$ in its window $\mathcal{W}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$ of \eqref{eq: window}.
\end{enumerate}
Then the sum $\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}$ of \eqref{eq: configuration sum} has an asymptotic formula.
\emph{(Proof in Appendix~\ref{app: sieve}.)}
\end{lemma}

The pieces in (b) of Lemma~\ref{lem: decoupling} are the \emph{straddling pieces} of $\mathcal{C}$, and the pieces in (c) are its \emph{window pieces}. Summed over $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$ and $\lambda'$, the straddling and window pieces over $\mathcal{B}$ satisfy the bound \eqref{eq: diagonal bound} of Appendix~\ref{app: sieve}, and their asymptotic formulas carry this bound to $\mathcal{A}$.

Lemma~\ref{lem: pieces} below concerns the pieces of hypotheses (a) to (c) of Lemma~\ref{lem: decoupling}. A piece with $\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) = \emptyset$ vanishes, so only pieces with $\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) \neq \emptyset$ are considered. A piece has the primes $\varpi_1, \dots, \varpi_K$, with $K = k$ for $\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\mathcal{E}; y)$, and with $K = k + 1$ and $\varpi_{k+1} = \varpi'$ for $\mathcal{P}'_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\lambda'}(\mathcal{E})$. Its cofactor $t$ is sifted at the constant level $y$, with $y = 1$ for $\mathcal{P}'_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\lambda'}(\mathcal{E})$. The norm of $\varpi_i$ lies in $\Lambda_i$, and the \emph{exponents} of the piece are the $x_i$, where
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: piece exponents}
\Lambda_i := \mathcal{I}_i \cap J_{\lambda_i} \quad (i \Le k), \qquad \Lambda_{k+1} := J_{\lambda'}, \qquad x_i := \frac{\log \inf \Lambda_i}{\log X}.
\end{equation}
For $1 \Le i \Le K$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: piece intervals}
\sup \Lambda_i \Le (1 + 1/H) \inf \Lambda_i, \qquad \inf \Lambda_i \Ge \mu^4/2.
\end{equation}
The bound for $\sup \Lambda_i$ holds by \eqref{eq: short intervals}. The bound for $\inf \Lambda_i$ holds for $i \Le k$ since $\Lambda_i \subset \mathcal{I}_i \subset [\mu^4, X]$, and for $i = k + 1$ since $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$ satisfies all constraints and $\lambda' \in \mathcal{W}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$, which give $\xi_{\lambda'} \Ge \mu^4/2$ by \eqref{eq: window floor}. So each $\log|\varpi_i|^2/\log X$ lies within $\mathcal{O}(1/(H \log X))$ of $x_i$, and a condition that holds at $(x_i)$ with margin $\varepsilon$ holds with margin $\varepsilon/2$ for every tuple of the piece, once $X$ is large.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem: pieces}
Let $C_1 \Ge 1$. A piece \eqref{eq: pieces} has an asymptotic formula, uniformly in $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$ and $\lambda'$, if one of the following holds.
\begin{enumerate}[(P1)]
\item $y = 1$, and either the exponents \eqref{eq: piece exponents} satisfy $\sum_{i=1}^{K} x_i \Le \tau - \varepsilon$, or the indices split as $\mathcal{U} \sqcup \mathcal{V}$ and the sums \eqref{eq: index sums} satisfy
\begin{equation*}
x_{\mathcal{U}} \Le \zeta - \varepsilon, \qquad x_{\mathcal{V}} \Le \rho - \varepsilon, \qquad \sum_{i=1}^{K} x_i \Le \nu - \varepsilon.
\end{equation*}
\item $y = \mu^4$, and the exponents satisfy one of the alternatives of (P1).
\item There are an index $i_0$ and a non-empty set $\mathcal{S}$ of other indices such that
\begin{equation*}
\inf \Bigl| \prod_{i \in \mathcal{S}} \varpi_i \Bigr|^2 \Ge \frac{X^{\sigma}}{C_1}, \qquad \frac{X/2}{\sup |\varpi_{i_0} \prod_{i \in \mathcal{S}} \varpi_i|^2} \Ge \frac{X^{\sigma}}{C_1},
\end{equation*}
with the infimum and the supremum over the tuples of the piece.
\item Every product $n = \varpi_1 \cdots \varpi_K$ of the piece satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: P4 large}
X^{\sigma}/C_1 \Le |n|^2 \Le X^{\tau - \varepsilon}, \qquad y \Le C_1 X^{\tau}/|n|^2,
\end{equation}
or
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: P4 small}
|n|^2 < X^{\sigma}, \qquad y \Le C_1\, z(|n|^2).
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
In (P1), $y = 1$, and the piece is $\sum_{v \neq 0} c_v |\mathcal{E}_v|$ by \eqref{eq: pieces} and \eqref{eq: cofactor sieve}, where $c_v$ is the number of tuples of the piece with product $v$. This $c_v$ is non-negative and at most $d(v)^K$ by Lemma~\ref{lem: counting}(c), and it is supported on $|v|^2 \Le X^{\sum_{i=1}^{K} x_i + o(1)}$ by \eqref{eq: piece exponents} and \eqref{eq: piece intervals}. By \eqref{eq: tuple set}, \eqref{eq: pieces} and \eqref{eq: piece exponents}, the tuples of the piece are all the tuples with $|\varpi_i|^2 \in \Lambda_i$ for every $i$. So, when the indices split as $\mathcal{U} \sqcup \mathcal{V}$, $c_v = \sum_{v = uu'} a_u b_{u'}$, where $a_u$ is the number of subtuples $(\varpi_i)_{i \in \mathcal{U}}$ with product $u$, and $b_{u'}$ is the number of subtuples $(\varpi_i)_{i \in \mathcal{V}}$ with product $u'$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: counting}(c) and \eqref{eq: piece intervals}, $a_u$ and $b_{u'}$ are non-negative, divisor-bounded and supported on at most $2$ dyadic ranges. Lemma~\ref{lem: type I} applies, since the margin $\varepsilon/2$ absorbs the factor $\mu^{-1}$: in case (a) when $\sum_{i=1}^{K} x_i \Le \tau - \varepsilon$, and in case (b), on each of the at most $4$ pairs of dyadic ranges of $u$ and $u'$, when the indices split as $\mathcal{U} \sqcup \mathcal{V}$.

In (P2), by \eqref{eq: pieces}, the piece is $\sum_{u \neq 0} c_u S'(\mathcal{E}_u, \mu^4)$, where $c_u$ is the number of tuples of the piece with product $u$. This $c_u$ is non-negative and divisor-bounded, as in (P1). Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental} applies with the constant function $z_0 \equiv \mu^4$ and $\varepsilon_0 = \varepsilon/4$, since the margin $\varepsilon/2$ absorbs the factors $X^{\varepsilon_0}$ and $\mu^{-1}$: in case (a) when $\sum_{i=1}^{K} x_i \Le \tau - \varepsilon$, and in case (b) when the indices split as $\mathcal{U} \sqcup \mathcal{V}$, with $u_1$ and $u_2$ the products of the primes indexed by $\mathcal{U}$ and by $\mathcal{V}$, each in at most $2$ dyadic ranges.

In (P3), by \eqref{eq: pieces} and \eqref{eq: cofactor sieve}, the piece is
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{u \neq 0,\ v \neq 0} a_u b_v\, \#\bigl\{ \varpi_{i_0} : |\varpi_{i_0}|^2 \in \Lambda_{i_0},\ uv\varpi_{i_0} \in \mathcal{E} \bigr\},
\end{equation*}
where $a_u$ is the number of subtuples $(\varpi_i)_{i \in \mathcal{S}}$ with product $u$, and $b_v$ is the number of pairs of a subtuple $(\varpi_i)_{i \notin \mathcal{S} \cup \{i_0\}}$ and a cofactor $t$ sifted at the level $y$ with product $v$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: counting}(c), as in (P1), the coefficients $a_u$ and $b_v$ are non-negative and divisor-bounded. Each $|\varpi_i|^2$ varies by at most the factor $1 + 1/H$ on the piece by \eqref{eq: piece intervals}, and $X/2 \Le |uv\varpi_{i_0}|^2 < X$ by \eqref{eq: sets}, so $u$ lies in at most $2$ dyadic ranges and $v$ in at most $4$. By (P3), $|u|^2 \Ge X^{\sigma}/C_1$ and $|v|^2 \Ge (X/2)/\sup|u\varpi_{i_0}|^2 \Ge X^{\sigma}/C_1$, so the dyadic ranges $u \sim U$ and $v \sim V$ that occur have $U, V \Ge X^{\sigma}/(2C_1)$. The norm of $\varpi_{i_0}$ ranges over $\Lambda_{i_0}$, with $\inf \Lambda_{i_0} \Ge \mu^4/2$ by \eqref{eq: piece intervals}, so Lemma~\ref{lem: type II} applies with $2C_1$ in place of $C_1$.

In (P4), by \eqref{eq: pieces} and \eqref{eq: cofactor sieve}, the piece is $\sum_{n \neq 0} a_n S'(\mathcal{E}_n, y)$, where $a_n$ is the number of tuples of the piece with product $n$. This $a_n$ is non-negative and divisor-bounded, as in (P1), and the level $y$ of the piece is at least $1$. Lemma~\ref{lem: level large} applies to the $n$ that satisfy \eqref{eq: P4 large}, and Lemma~\ref{lem: level small} to the other $n$, which satisfy \eqref{eq: P4 small}, both with $C_l = C_1$ and the constant level $y$. In each of (P1) to (P4), the asymptotic formula of the piece holds uniformly in $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$ and $\lambda'$ by the convention of Section~\ref{sec: notation} on implied constants.
\end{proof}

\section{Sums of sifting functions over two primes}
\label{sec: two primes}

Throughout this section, $p \sim P$ and $q \sim Q$, and the pairs $(p, q)$ form a box. The lemmas of this section treat the sum $\sum^*$ of \eqref{eq: identity 37} on boxes and supply the treatments of Table~\ref{tab: ranges}. Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 11} gives the sum of $S(\mathcal{A}_{pq}, q)$ over a whole box an asymptotic formula. Lemma~\ref{lem: three primes} gives the sum \eqref{eq: lemma 12 sum} over $(p, q, r)$ an asymptotic formula under (a) or (b) of \eqref{eq: lemma 12 cases}. Lemmas~\ref{lem: lemma 13} and~\ref{lem: lemma 14} split the sum of $S(\mathcal{A}_{pq}, q)$ over the pairs of a box with $q \prec p$ as $S_< + S_\Ge$ in \eqref{eq: lemma 13 split} and \eqref{eq: lemma 14 split} according to the size of $|q|^2$, give $S_<$ an asymptotic formula, and express $S_\Ge$ by identity \eqref{eq: identity 35} for $PQ \Ge X^{\sigma}$ and by identity \eqref{eq: identity 36} for $PQ < X^{\sigma}$. Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 13} also gives the sum over $(p, q)$ in \eqref{eq: identity 35} an asymptotic formula. Under (a) or (b) of \eqref{eq: lemma 12 cases}, Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 13} gives the sum over $(p, q, r)$ in \eqref{eq: identity 35} an asymptotic formula too. Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 14} also gives the sums over $(p, q)$ and over $(p, q, r)$ in \eqref{eq: identity 36} asymptotic formulas.

In every sifting function $S(\mathcal{A}_u, \cdot)$ below, every prime factor of $u$ satisfies the condition that the level imposes, so $S(\mathcal{A}_u, \cdot) = S'(\mathcal{A}_u, \cdot)$ by \eqref{eq: sifting} and \eqref{eq: cofactor sieve}. The sums and identities of this section are written for $\mathcal{A}$. Each sum is defined for $\mathcal{B}$ in the same way, and every identity holds with $\mathcal{B}$ in place of $\mathcal{A}$, since \eqref{eq: buchstab} holds for $\mathcal{E} = \mathcal{A}$ and for $\mathcal{E} = \mathcal{B}$.

In the proofs below, Lemma~\ref{lem: pieces} is applied with $C_1 = 10$, and with $\varepsilon/2$ in place of $\varepsilon$, so that a constant factor in a bound for a norm is absorbed by $X^{\varepsilon/2}$. Since $\sigma + \tau = 1$ by \eqref{eq: parameters}, a piece then satisfies the conditions of (P3) if, for an index $i_0$ and a non-empty set $\mathcal{S}$ of other indices, each of its tuples satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: P3 criterion}
\Bigl| \prod_{i \in \mathcal{S}} \varpi_i \Bigr|^2 \Ge X^{\sigma}, \qquad \Bigl| \varpi_{i_0} \prod_{i \in \mathcal{S}} \varpi_i \Bigr|^2 \Le \frac{C_1 X^{1 - \sigma}}{2} = 5X^{\tau}.
\end{equation}

The ranges of the configurations below lie in $[\mu^4, X]$, as Definition~\ref{def: configuration} requires. Each lower limit is $\mu^4$, is at least $X^{\varepsilon}$ by the hypotheses of the lemma being proved and the inequality $2\theta < \sigma$ of Table~\ref{tab: inequalities}, or is $z(PQ)$, which exceeds $X^{\theta}$ by \eqref{eq: z lower}, since $PQ < X^{\sigma}$ in Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 14}. In Lemmas~\ref{lem: three primes} to~\ref{lem: lemma 14}, the lower limit $P$ of $|p|^2$ is at least $X^{\varepsilon}$, since $Q \Le 2P$, $PQ \Ge X^{\sigma - 2\theta}$ and $2\theta < \sigma$ give, for small $\varepsilon$,
\begin{equation*}
P^2 \Ge \frac{PQ}{2} \Ge \frac{X^{\sigma - 2\theta}}{2} \Ge X^{2\varepsilon}.
\end{equation*}
Each upper limit is at most $2P$, $2Q$, $(X/(PQ))^{1/2}$ or $X^{\tau}/(PQ)$, and each of these is below $X$, since $P, Q \Ge 1$, $PQ \Le X^{\nu}$ in Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 11}, $PQ \Le X^{\tau}$ in Lemmas~\ref{lem: three primes} to~\ref{lem: lemma 14}, and $\tau, \nu < 1$. By the convention of Section~\ref{sec: notation} on implied constants, the asymptotic formulas of Lemmas~\ref{lem: lemma 11} to~\ref{lem: lemma 14} hold uniformly in $P$ and $Q$, and the asymptotic formula of Lemma~\ref{lem: three primes} also holds uniformly in the intervals $\mathcal{I}_q$ and $\mathcal{I}_r$ of its statement.

Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 11} supplies the treatment of Range~1 in Table~\ref{tab: ranges}. The proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 11} applies Buchstab's identity \eqref{eq: buchstab} at the level $\mu^4$ in \eqref{eq: lemma 11 buchstab}. The linear estimate, Lemma~\ref{lem: type I}, treats the sum sifted at the level $\mu^4$ through Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental}. The bilinear estimate, Lemma~\ref{lem: type II}, treats the subtracted sum of $S'(\mathcal{A}_{pqr}, r)$, with $p$ as the variable $u$ of Lemma~\ref{lem: type II}.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem: lemma 11}
Suppose that $X^{\sigma} \Le P \Le X^{\zeta - \varepsilon}$, $Q \Ge P X^{-2\theta}$ and $PQ \Le X^{\nu - \varepsilon}$. Then the sum of $S(\mathcal{A}_{pq}, q)$ over $p \sim P$ and $q \sim Q$ has an asymptotic formula.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Since $\nu - \sigma = \rho$ by \eqref{eq: parameters}, the bounds $PQ \Le X^{\nu - \varepsilon}$ and $P \Ge X^{\sigma}$ give $Q \Le X^{\rho - \varepsilon}$. With the hypothesis $Q \Ge P X^{-2\theta}$, and with $2\theta < \sigma$ and $\rho < \sigma$ from Table~\ref{tab: inequalities}, every pair of the box satisfies
\begin{equation*}
\mu^4 < X^{\sigma - 2\theta} \Le P X^{-2\theta} \Le Q \Le |q|^2 < 2Q \Le 2X^{\rho - \varepsilon} < X^{\sigma} \Le P \Le |p|^2.
\end{equation*}
Hence $q \prec p$, and $S(\mathcal{A}_{pq}, q) = S'(\mathcal{A}_{pq}, q)$. Since $|q|^2 > \mu^4$, Buchstab's identity \eqref{eq: buchstab} at the level $\mu^4$ gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: lemma 11 buchstab}
\sum_{p \sim P,\, q \sim Q} S'(\mathcal{A}_{pq}, q) = \sum_{p \sim P,\, q \sim Q} S'(\mathcal{A}_{pq}, \mu^4) - \sum_{p \sim P,\, q \sim Q} \sum_{r \prec q,\ |r|^2 \Ge \mu^4} S'(\mathcal{A}_{pqr}, r).
\end{equation}
The sum of $S'(\mathcal{A}_{pq}, \mu^4)$ in \eqref{eq: lemma 11 buchstab} is, in the sense of Definition~\ref{def: configuration}, the configuration with variables $p, q$, ranges $[P, 2P)$ and $[Q, 2Q)$, no constraints, and the sieve condition of type (C). This configuration has no straddling pieces, since it has no constraints, and no window pieces, since the window \eqref{eq: window} is empty in type (C). Each piece of hypothesis (a) of Lemma~\ref{lem: decoupling} has the level $y = \mu^4$ by \eqref{eq: piece level} and falls under (P2) of Lemma~\ref{lem: pieces} with $\mathcal{U} = \{p\}$ and $\mathcal{V} = \{q\}$, since
\begin{equation*}
|p|^2 < 2P \Le 2X^{\zeta - \varepsilon}, \qquad |q|^2 < 2Q \Le 2X^{\rho - \varepsilon}, \qquad |pq|^2 < 4PQ \Le 4X^{\nu - \varepsilon}.
\end{equation*}

The bound $|r|^2 < 2Q$, from $r \prec q$, gives only $|pr|^2 < 4PQ$, which may exceed the bound $5X^{\tau}$ of \eqref{eq: P3 criterion}, since $PQ$ may be as large as $X^{\nu - \varepsilon}$ and $\nu > \tau$. So (P3) needs a smaller upper bound for $|r|^2$. In the sum of $S'(\mathcal{A}_{pqr}, r)$ in \eqref{eq: lemma 11 buchstab}, only the primes $r$ with $|r|^4 < X/(PQ)$ contribute. Indeed, suppose that $|r|^4 \Ge X/(PQ)$. Let $pqrt$ be an element counted by $S'(\mathcal{A}_{pqr}, r)$ or by $S'(\mathcal{B}_{pqr}, r)$, with cofactor $t$. If $t \neq 1$, then $t$ has only prime factors $\succcurlyeq r$ by \eqref{eq: cofactor sieve}, so $|t|^2 \Ge |r|^2$. Then $|pqrt|^2 \Ge PQ |r|^4 \Ge X$, so $pqrt$ lies neither in $\mathcal{A}$ nor in $\mathcal{B}$ by \eqref{eq: sets}. If $t = 1$, then $X/2 \Le |pqr|^2 < 4PQ|r|^2$ by \eqref{eq: sets}, hence $|r|^2 > X/(8PQ)$. Since $\nu + \rho = (2 + 8\theta)/3 < 1$ for $\theta < 1/8$ by \eqref{eq: parameters}, the bounds $PQ \Le X^{\nu - \varepsilon}$ and $Q \Le X^{\rho - \varepsilon}$ give
\begin{equation*}
16PQ^2 \Le 16X^{\nu + \rho - 2\varepsilon} < X, \qquad |r|^2 > \frac{X}{8PQ} = \frac{X}{16PQ^2} \cdot 2Q > 2Q > |q|^2.
\end{equation*}
The bound $|r|^2 > |q|^2$ contradicts $r \prec q$.
