\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.8\textwidth]{../Code/figures/total_loss.pdf}
\caption{The total loss of each decomposition of Table~\ref{tab: roots}, computed by the first method of Section~\ref{sec: numerics} at $61$ values of $\theta$ from $0.112$ to $0.118$. The dashed line marks the total loss $1$, and the dots mark the values of $\theta$ in Table~\ref{tab: roots}.}
\label{fig: total loss}
\end{figure}

The computation is not carried out in interval arithmetic, so its accuracy is checked by refining the quadrature of the first method and by comparing the methods. The finest rule tested for the first method has $512$ panels on the $\alpha$-axis for the regions $\mathsf{D}_k$, $32$ panels per axis for the regions $\mathsf{E}_k$, and $12$ nodes per subinterval. Between the rule used for Table~\ref{tab: losses} and this finest rule, the losses at $\theta = 0.1129$ change by at most $2.5 \times 10^{-9}$, and their total by $2.6 \times 10^{-10}$. The $8$ coarser rules tested have $6$ to $12$ nodes per subinterval and $2$ to $16$ panels per axis for the regions $\mathsf{E}_k$, with $16$ times as many panels on the $\alpha$-axis for the regions $\mathsf{D}_k$. As the number of nodes per subinterval times the number of panels per axis grows from $12$ to $192$, the total differs from that of the finest rule by amounts that decrease from $8.7 \times 10^{-6}$ to $6.8 \times 10^{-11}$. The unrounded totals of the methods differ by $1.2 \times 10^{-6}$, which is $1.1$ times the unrounded error estimate of the second method in Table~\ref{tab: losses}. Both quantities come almost entirely from $\mathsf{E}_{10}$. The gap $1 - 0.9990033 = 9.967 \times 10^{-4}$ exceeds this difference, this error estimate and the change $2.6 \times 10^{-10}$ of the total from the rule of Table~\ref{tab: losses} to the finest rule by a factor of more than $800$.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm: sector}]
Let $\theta = 0.1129$. By Table~\ref{tab: losses}, $\Phi(\theta) < 1$, so Lemma~\ref{lem: lower bound} gives $S(\mathcal{A}, X^{1/2}) \gg X^{\tau}/\log X = X^{1/2 + \theta}/\log X$ by \eqref{eq: parameters}, uniformly in $\psi$. By \eqref{eq: sets} and \eqref{eq: sifting}, $S(\mathcal{A}, X^{1/2})$ is the number of Gaussian primes in $\mathcal{A}$, so this is \eqref{eq: sector bound}.
\end{proof}

With the polytope $\mathsf{P}$ of \eqref{eq: polytope} in place of $\mathsf{L}$ (Definition~\ref{def: total loss}), the first and second methods give $\Phi_{\mathsf{P}}(0.1133) = 0.9978243$ and $0.9978250$, so the same argument gives \eqref{eq: sector bound} with $\theta = 0.1133$. As in the proof of Theorem~\ref{thm: primes}, \eqref{eq: fractional bound} then gives $\{\sqrt{p}\} < p^{-0.2734}$ for infinitely many primes.

\section{Conclusion}
\label{sec: conclusion}

Of the total loss $0.999$ at $\theta = 0.1129$, the regions $\mathsf{D}_1$, $\mathsf{D}_2$ and $\mathsf{D}_4$ account for $0.877$. On these regions, after Buchstab's identity \eqref{eq: buchstab} introduces a third prime $r \prec q$, no factorization of $pqr$ satisfies the conditions \eqref{eq: linear exponents} of the linear estimate once $|r|^2$ is close to $|q|^2$. We have not found another grouping that gives the sums over these regions asymptotic formulas. A further improvement may need new arithmetic information for the Hecke characters $\lambda^m$ of \eqref{eq: hecke character}. \citet[p.~134]{harman2001gaussian} call the improvement of their arithmetic information ``the more interesting challenge''. The numerical evaluation of the losses could be made rigorous by interval arithmetic.

\section*{Data availability}

The code that computes every number in Section~\ref{sec: numerics}, Table~\ref{tab: hl 0119} and Appendix~\ref{app: inequalities}, and the data of Figures~\ref{fig: domain} and~\ref{fig: total loss}, are in the folder \texttt{Code} of the repository \url{https://github.com/xuda-ye-math/Gaussian-Prime}. The code is written in Julia~1.13, except for a Python script that draws the figures, and the file \texttt{Manifest.toml} of the folder fixes the versions of its Julia packages. The file \texttt{README.md} of the folder describes each computation and its output.

\appendix

\section{Proofs of the linear and bilinear estimates}
\label{app: arithmetic}
\label{sec: arithmetic}

This appendix derives Lemmas~\ref{lem: type I} and~\ref{lem: type II} of Section~\ref{sec: estimates} from the arithmetic information. It begins with standard facts, which Sections~\ref{sec: decoupling}, \ref{sec: decomposition} and~\ref{sec: integrals} and Appendices~\ref{app: sieve} and~\ref{app: integrals} also use. Lemma~\ref{lem: prime ideal theorem} bounds sums over Gaussian primes, and Lemma~\ref{lem: counting} counts Gaussian integers and their divisors. Lemma~\ref{lem: perron} is the truncated Perron formula used in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: type II}.

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem: prime ideal theorem}
The number $\pi_G(y)$ of Gaussian primes of norm less than $y$ satisfies $\pi_G(y) = \mathrm{Li}(y) + \mathcal{O}(y \exp(-c (\log y)^{1/2}))$ for some $c > 0$, where $\mathrm{Li}(y) := \int_2^y dt/\log t$. Consequently, for fixed $B > 0$ and with $H$ of \eqref{eq: short intervals}, the following hold once $X$ is large.
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
\item For fixed $\kappa \Ge 1$ and every $Y \Ge \mu$,
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{Y \Le |\varpi|^2 < Y(1 + 1/H)^{\kappa}} |\varpi|^{-2} \Le \frac{2\kappa}{H \log Y}.
\end{equation*}
\item $\sum_{|\varpi|^2 < y} |\varpi|^{-2} \Le \log\log y + C$ for $y \Ge 3$.
\item For fixed $a_0 > 0$, uniformly for $a_0 \Le a < b \Le 1$,
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{X^a \Le |\varpi|^2 < X^b} |\varpi|^{-2} = \log\frac{b}{a} + \mathcal{O}\Bigl( \frac{1}{\log X} \Bigr).
\end{equation*}
\item $\pi_G(y) - \pi_G(y/2) = (1 + \mathcal{O}(1/\log y))\, y/(2\log y)$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For $a \in \{1, 3\}$, let $\pi(y; 4, a)$ be the number of rational primes $\ell < y$ with $\ell \equiv a \pmod 4$. A Gaussian prime is $1 + i$, a prime of norm $\ell$ for a rational prime $\ell \equiv 1 \pmod 4$, or a rational prime $\ell \equiv 3 \pmod 4$, of norm $\ell^2$. Hence $\pi_G(y) = 2\pi(y; 4, 1) + \pi(y^{1/2}; 4, 3) + \mathcal{O}(1)$. The formula for $\pi_G(y)$ follows by partial summation from the prime number theorem in progressions modulo $4$ \citep[Section~1.2]{thorner2024refinements}, in the form
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{\ell \Le x,\ \ell \equiv a \,(4)} \log \ell = \frac{x}{2} \Bigl( 1 + \mathcal{O}\bigl( \exp(-c' (\log x)^{3/5} (\log\log x)^{-1/5}) \bigr) \Bigr), \qquad a \in \{1, 3\},
\end{equation*}
with a constant $c' > 0$. Statements (b) and (c) follow by partial summation, and (d) follows from the formula for $\pi_G(y)$ and
\begin{equation*}
\mathrm{Li}(y) - \mathrm{Li}(y/2) = \int_{y/2}^{y} \frac{dt}{\log t} = \Bigl( 1 + \mathcal{O}\Bigl( \frac{1}{\log y} \Bigr) \Bigr) \frac{y}{2\log y},
\end{equation*}
since $1/\log t = (1 + \mathcal{O}(1/\log y))/\log y$ for $y/2 \Le t \Le y$. In (a), the main term contributes
\begin{equation*}
\log\Bigl( 1 + \frac{\kappa \log(1 + 1/H)}{\log Y} \Bigr) \Le \frac{\kappa}{H \log Y},
\end{equation*}
and the error term contributes $\mathcal{O}(\exp(-c (\log Y)^{1/2}))$. Since $Y \Ge \mu$, \eqref{eq: parameters} gives $\log Y \Ge (\log X)^{4/5}$, so $H = (\log X)^B \Le (\log Y)^{5B/4}$. Hence the error term is smaller than $1/(H \log Y)$ for all $Y \Ge \mu$ once $X$ is large, and (a) follows from $\kappa \Ge 1$.
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem: counting}
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
\item For $M \Ge 1$, the number of Gaussian integers $v$ with $X/(2M) \Le |v|^2 < X/M$ is at most $C X/M$. In particular $|\mathcal{B}_u| \Le C X/|u|^2$, since the cofactor $n/u$ of each $n \in \mathcal{B}_u$ satisfies $X/(2|u|^2) \Le |n/u|^2 < X/|u|^2$.
\item For $k \Ge 1$ and $Y \Ge 2$,
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{|n|^2 \Le Y} d(n)^k \ll_k Y (\log Y)^{2^k}, \qquad \sum_{|n|^2 \Le Y} d(n)^k |n|^{-2} \ll_k (\log Y)^{2^k}.
\end{equation*}
\item A Gaussian integer $n$ has at most $d(n)^{h - 1}$ ordered factorizations into $h$ factors.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
For (a), the count is $0$ if $X/M < 1$, and $\pi X/(8M) + \mathcal{O}((X/M)^{1/2} + 1)$ otherwise. For (b), $d$ is multiplicative with $d(\varpi^e) = e + 1$. So, by Lemma~\ref{lem: prime ideal theorem}(b), the sum of $d(n)^k |n|^{-2}$ is at most
\begin{equation*}
\prod_{|\varpi|^2 \Le Y} \sum_{e \Ge 0} (e + 1)^k |\varpi|^{-2e} \Le \exp\Bigl( \sum_{|\varpi|^2 \Le Y} \bigl( 2^k |\varpi|^{-2} + C_k |\varpi|^{-4} \bigr) \Bigr) \ll_k (\log Y)^{2^k},
\end{equation*}
and the sum of $d(n)^k$ is at most $Y$ times the sum of $d(n)^k |n|^{-2}$. For (c), the number $d_h(n)$ of ordered factorizations satisfies, by induction on $h$,
\begin{equation*}
d_h(n) = \sum_{u \mid n} d_{h-1}(n/u) \Le d(n)^{h-1}.
\end{equation*}
\end{proof}

\begin{lemma}
\label{lem: perron}
For $y > 0$, $c > 0$ and $T_0 \Ge c$, let
\begin{equation*}
I(y) := \frac{1}{2\pi i} \int_{c - iT_0}^{c + iT_0} y^s\, \frac{ds}{s}.
\end{equation*}
Then $|I(y)| \Le y^c \log(3T_0/c)$, and $|I(y) - \mathbf{1}[y > 1]| \Le y^c/(\pi T_0 |\log y|)$ for $y \neq 1$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
The bound $|I(y)| \Le y^c \log(3T_0/c)$ follows from
\begin{equation*}
|I(y)| \Le \frac{y^c}{2\pi} \int_{-T_0}^{T_0} \frac{dt}{(c^2 + t^2)^{1/2}} = \frac{y^c}{\pi} \log\frac{T_0 + (c^2 + T_0^2)^{1/2}}{c} \Le \frac{y^c}{\pi} \log\frac{3T_0}{c},
\end{equation*}
since $T_0 \Ge c$ gives $T_0 + (c^2 + T_0^2)^{1/2} \Le (1 + \sqrt{2})\, T_0 < 3T_0$. For $y > 1$, move the segment to $\mathrm{Re}\, s = -K$ and let $K \to \infty$. The residue at $0$ is $1$, the integral over $\mathrm{Re}\, s = -K$ tends to $0$, and each horizontal side is at most
\begin{equation*}
\frac{1}{2\pi T_0} \int_{-\infty}^{c} y^u\, du = \frac{y^c}{2\pi T_0 \log y}.
\end{equation*}
For $y < 1$, move the segment to $\mathrm{Re}\, s = K$, where there is no pole, and obtain the same bound with $|\log y|$.
\end{proof}

For an integer $m$ and a Gaussian integer $n \neq 0$ put, as in \citet[p.~122]{harman2001gaussian},
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: hecke character}
\lambda^m(n) := \Bigl( \frac{n}{|n|} \Bigr)^{4m}.
\end{equation}
This is a Hecke character. It is unchanged when $n$ is multiplied by a unit, so it is well defined under the identification of associates fixed in Section~\ref{sec: introduction}, and it is completely multiplicative. Since $\lambda^m(n) = e^{4im \arg n}$, it detects the argument of $n$ modulo $\pi/2$. Lemmas~\ref{lem: sector inequality} to~\ref{lem: type I input} below are quoted without proof from \citet{harman2001gaussian}. The linear estimate, Lemma~\ref{lem: type I}, rests on Lemmas~\ref{lem: sector inequality} and~\ref{lem: type I input}, and the bilinear estimate, Lemma~\ref{lem: type II}, on Lemmas~\ref{lem: sector inequality}, \ref{lem: mean value} and~\ref{lem: hecke pnt}.

\begin{lemma}[\citet{harman2001gaussian}, (12)]
\label{lem: sector inequality}
For every $T \Ge 1$ and all non-negative weights $a_n$,
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{n \in \mathcal{A}} a_n = \frac{2}{\pi X^{\sigma}} \sum_{n \in \mathcal{B}} a_n + \mathcal{O}\Bigl( \frac{1}{T} \sum_{n \in \mathcal{B}} a_n \Bigr) + \mathcal{O}\Bigl( X^{-\sigma} \sum_{m = 1}^{T} \Bigl| \sum_{n \in \mathcal{B}} a_n \lambda^m(n) \Bigr| \Bigr).
\end{equation*}
\end{lemma}

Harman and Lewis state Lemma~\ref{lem: sector inequality} for any weights and note that it follows from \citet[Chapter~2]{baker1986diophantine}. It is quoted here for non-negative weights, the only ones used.

\begin{lemma}[\citet{harman2001gaussian}, Lemma~1]
\label{lem: mean value}
For $T \Ge 1$, $N \Ge 1$ and all coefficients $f(n)$,
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{m = 1}^{T} \Bigl| \sum_{|n|^2 \Le N} f(n) \lambda^m(n) \Bigr|^2 \ll (T + N) \sideset{}{'}\sum_{|n|^2 \Le N} |f_1(n)|^2, \qquad f_1(n) := \sum_{\arg u = \arg n} f(u),
\end{equation*}
where $\sum'$ runs over the primitive Gaussian integers $n = x + iy$, $\gcd(x, y) = 1$.
\end{lemma}

\begin{lemma}[\citet{harman2001gaussian}, Lemma~4]
\label{lem: hecke pnt}
Let $R, T_0 \Ge 2$, $1 \Le m \Le T$ and $|t| \Le T_0$, and suppose that
\begin{equation*}
T \Ge S \Ge R + \exp\bigl( (\log(T T_0))^{4/5} \bigr).
\end{equation*}
Then
\begin{equation*}
\Bigl| \sum_{R \Le |\varpi|^2 < S} |\varpi|^{2it} \lambda^m(\varpi) \Bigr| \ll S \exp\Bigl( -\frac{\log S}{2 (\log(T T_0))^{7/10}} \Bigr).
\end{equation*}
\end{lemma}

Lemma~\ref{lem: mean value} is \citet[Lemma~6]{coleman1993rosser}, and Lemma~\ref{lem: hecke pnt} rests on the zero-free region of \citet{coleman1990zero}.

\begin{lemma}[\citet{harman2001gaussian}, Lemmas~6 and~7]
\label{lem: type I input}
Let $A > 0$, $T := X^{\sigma} (\log X)^A$, and let $a_u$, $b_v$ be divisor-bounded.
\begin{enumerate}[(a)]
\item If $U \Le X^{\tau} \mu^{-1}$, then
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{m = 1}^{T} \Bigl| \sum_{u \sim U} a_u \sum_{ut \in \mathcal{B}} \lambda^m(ut) \Bigr| \ll X (\log X)^{-A}.
\end{equation*}
\item If $U \Le X^{\zeta} \mu^{-1}$, $V \Le X^{\rho} \mu^{-1}$ and $UV \Le X^{\nu} \mu^{-1}$, then
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{m = 1}^{T} \Bigl| \sum_{u \sim U} a_u \sum_{v \sim V} b_v \sum_{uvt \in \mathcal{B}} \lambda^m(uvt) \Bigr| \ll X (\log X)^{-A}.
\end{equation*}
\end{enumerate}
Here $t$ runs over all Gaussian integers with the stated product in $\mathcal{B}$.
\end{lemma}

Remark~\ref{rem: annulus} in Appendix~\ref{app: comparison} explains the conditions $ut \in \mathcal{B}$ and $uvt \in \mathcal{B}$ of Lemma~\ref{lem: type I input}.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: type I}]
With $c'_n := \sum_{v \mid n} c_v \Ge 0$,
\begin{equation*}
\sum_v c_v |\mathcal{A}_v| = \sum_{n \in \mathcal{A}} c'_n, \qquad \sum_{n \in \mathcal{B}} c'_n = \sum_v c_v |\mathcal{B}_v|.
\end{equation*}
Apply Lemma~\ref{lem: sector inequality} with $a_n = c'_n$ and $T = X^{\sigma} (\log X)^A$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: counting}(a) and~(b), its error term $\mathcal{O}(T^{-1} \sum_{n \in \mathcal{B}} c'_n)$ is at most
\begin{equation*}
X^{-\sigma} (\log X)^{-A} \cdot C X \sum_v c_v |v|^{-2} \ll X^{\tau} (\log X)^{C - A}.
\end{equation*}
The error term of Lemma~\ref{lem: sector inequality} with the sums over $\lambda^m$ is
\begin{equation*}
X^{-\sigma} \sum_{m \Le T} \Bigl| \sum_v c_v \sum_{vt \in \mathcal{B}} \lambda^m(vt) \Bigr|.
\end{equation*}
Under (a), after the range of $v$ is split into $\mathcal{O}(\log X)$ dyadic ranges, it is $\ll X^{\tau} (\log X)^{1 - A}$ by Lemma~\ref{lem: type I input}(a). Under (b) it is $\ll X^{\tau} (\log X)^{-A}$ by Lemma~\ref{lem: type I input}(b), with $u'$ as its variable $v$ and $U'$ as its $V$. Since $A$ is arbitrary, the lemma follows.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: type II}]
The proof separates the variables by Lemma~\ref{lem: perron} and bounds the resulting sums by Lemmas~\ref{lem: mean value} and~\ref{lem: hecke pnt}.

Fix $A$, let $T = X^{\sigma} (\log X)^A$ be as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: type I}, and let $E_m$ be the sum of $a_u b_v \lambda^m(uv\varpi)$ over the triples counted in \eqref{eq: type II} with $\mathcal{E} = \mathcal{B}$. As in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: type I}, it suffices to show that $\sum_{m \Le T} |E_m| \ll X (\log X)^{-A}$. A triple occurs only if $X/(8UV) \Le |\varpi|^2 < X/(UV)$. Put $Z_1' := \max(Z_1, X/(8UV))$ and $Z_2' := \min(Z_2, X/(UV))$, and assume that $Z_1' < Z_2'$, since otherwise $E_m = 0$. Then
\begin{equation*}
E_m = \sum_{u \sim U,\ v \sim V}\ \sum_{Z_1' \Le |\varpi|^2 < Z_2'} a_u b_v \lambda^m(uv\varpi)\, \mathbf{1}[X/2 \Le |uv\varpi|^2 < X],
\end{equation*}
and each triple in this sum has $|uv\varpi|^2 < 4X$.

For $N = |uv\varpi|^2 < 4X$, write
\begin{equation*}
\mathbf{1}[X/2 \Le N < X] = \mathbf{1}[X/N > 1] - \mathbf{1}[(X/2)/N > 1],
\end{equation*}
and apply Lemma~\ref{lem: perron} to $y = Y/N$ for $Y \in \{X, X/2\}$, with $c = 1/\log X$ and $T_0 = X^3$. Since $N \Ge 1$ and $Y \Le X$, $y^c \Le X^c = e$. If $|N - Y| \Ge 1/2$, then $|\log y| \Ge 1/(16X)$ by the mean value theorem, since $N, Y < 4X$. For these $N$, Lemma~\ref{lem: perron} bounds the error $|I(y) - \mathbf{1}[y > 1]|$ by $16eX/(\pi T_0)$. At most $2$ integers $N$ have $|N - Y| < 1/2$, and for them the error is at most $|I(y)| + 1 \Le e \log(3T_0/c) + 1$.

Each $N < 4X$ is the norm of at most $d(N)$ Gaussian integers, since each of them divides $N$, and by Lemma~\ref{lem: counting}(c) each of these is the product of at most $d(N)^2$ triples, each with weight $a_u b_v \Le d(N)^{2C_0}$. As a Gaussian integer, $N$ has norm $N^2 < 16X^2$, so Lemma~\ref{lem: counting}(b) with $Y = 16X^2$ gives $d(N)^h \ll_h X^2 (\log X)^{2^h}$, that is, $d(N) \ll_h X^{3/h}$, for every fixed $h \Ge 1$. So all these quantities are $X^{o(1)}$. Summed with these weights over the at most $4X$ integers $N$ with $|N - Y| \Ge 1/2$ and the at most $2$ others, the errors of Lemma~\ref{lem: perron} add up to
\begin{equation*}
4X \cdot X^{o(1)} \cdot \frac{16eX}{\pi T_0} + 2X^{o(1)} \bigl( e \log(3T_0/c) + 1 \bigr) = \mathcal{O}\bigl( X^{o(1)} \bigr) \qquad (T_0 = X^3).
\end{equation*}

Since $\lambda^m$ is completely multiplicative, Lemma~\ref{lem: perron} then gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: perron type II}
E_m = \frac{1}{2\pi i} \int_{c - iT_0}^{c + iT_0} \bigl(X^s - (X/2)^s\bigr) F_m(s) G_m(s) P_m(s)\, \frac{ds}{s} + \mathcal{O}(X^{o(1)}),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation*}
F_m(s) := \sum_{u \sim U} a_u \lambda^m(u) |u|^{-2s}, \qquad G_m(s) := \sum_{v \sim V} b_v \lambda^m(v) |v|^{-2s}, \qquad P_m(s) := \sum_{Z_1' \Le |\varpi|^2 < Z_2'} \lambda^m(\varpi) |\varpi|^{-2s}.
\end{equation*}
On the line $\mathrm{Re}\, s = c$, $|X^s - (X/2)^s| \Le 2e$, and $\int_{-T_0}^{T_0} |c + it|^{-1}\, dt \ll \log X$. So, summing \eqref{eq: perron type II} over $m \Le T$ and using $T X^{o(1)} = X^{\sigma + o(1)}$, we obtain
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: type II reduction}
\sum_{m \Le T} |E_m| \ll \log X \cdot \sup_{|t| \Le T_0} \sum_{m \Le T} \bigl| F_m(c + it)\, G_m(c + it)\, P_m(c + it) \bigr| + X^{\sigma + o(1)}.
\end{equation}

By Cauchy's inequality, the sum over $m$ in \eqref{eq: type II reduction} satisfies
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{m \Le T} \bigl| F_m(c + it)\, G_m(c + it)\, P_m(c + it) \bigr| \Le \max_{m \Le T} |P_m(c + it)| \Bigl( \sum_{m \Le T} |F_m(c + it)|^2 \Bigr)^{1/2} \Bigl( \sum_{m \Le T} |G_m(c + it)|^2 \Bigr)^{1/2}.
\end{equation*}

To bound $\sum_{m \Le T} |F_m(c + it)|^2$, apply Lemma~\ref{lem: mean value} with the norm bound $2U$ and with the coefficients $f(u) = a_u |u|^{-2s}$ for $u \sim U$ and $f(u) = 0$ otherwise. A Gaussian integer $u$ with $\arg u = \arg n$, $n$ primitive, is $u = kn$ with $k$ a positive rational integer. So $|f_1(n)| \Le \sum_k d(kn)^{C_0}$, where $k \Ge 1$ runs over the integers with $k^2 |n|^2 \in [U, 2U)$. This sum has at most $(2U)^{1/2}/|n|$ terms. By Cauchy's inequality and $d(kn) \Le d(k) d(n)$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: c1 bound}
\sideset{}{'}\sum_{n} |f_1(n)|^2 \Le (2U)^{1/2} \sum_{k^2 \Le 2U} d(k)^{2C_0} \sum_{|n|^2 < 2U/k^2} d(n)^{2C_0} |n|^{-1}.
\end{equation}
The sum of $d(n)^{2C_0} |n|^{-1}$ in \eqref{eq: c1 bound} is $\ll (U/k^2)^{1/2} (\log X)^C$, by Lemma~\ref{lem: counting}(b) and partial summation. Inserting this bound in \eqref{eq: c1 bound} gives
\begin{equation*}
\sideset{}{'}\sum_{n} |f_1(n)|^2 \ll U (\log X)^C \sum_{k \Le (2U)^{1/2}} \frac{d(k)^{2C_0}}{k}.
\end{equation*}
The sum over the rational integers $k$ is at most a product over the rational primes $\ell \Le (2U)^{1/2}$:
\begin{equation*}
\prod_{\ell \Le (2U)^{1/2}} \sum_{g \Ge 0} (g + 1)^{4C_0} \ell^{-g} \ll (\log X)^{2^{4C_0}}.
\end{equation*}
Here $d(\ell^g) \Le (g + 1)^2$ for every rational prime $\ell$, and $\sum_{\ell \Le x} 1/\ell \Le \log\log x + \mathcal{O}(1)$ follows from the prime number theorem modulo $4$. So $\sideset{}{'}\sum_{n} |f_1(n)|^2 \ll U (\log X)^C$, and, since $T \Le C_1 U (\log X)^A$, uniformly in $|t| \Le T_0$,
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{m \Le T} |F_m(c + it)|^2 \ll (T + U) U (\log X)^C \ll U^2 (\log X)^{A + C}.
\end{equation*}
The same bound holds for $\sum_{m \Le T} |G_m(c + it)|^2$ with $V$ in place of $U$.

It remains to bound $P_m(c + it)$. The weight $|\varpi|^{-2c}$ in its terms is decreasing in $|\varpi|^2$ and is at most $1$. By partial summation, $|P_m(c + it)|$ is at most $2$ times the largest value, over $Z \in [Z_1', Z_2']$, of
\begin{equation*}
\Bigl| \sum_{Z_1' \Le |\varpi|^2 < Z} \lambda^m(\varpi) |\varpi|^{-2it} \Bigr|.
\end{equation*}
Each of these sums is the difference of the sums over $2 \Le |\varpi|^2 < S$ for $S = Z$ and for $S = Z_1'$. Lemma~\ref{lem: hecke pnt}, with $-t$ in place of $t$, applies to them with $R = 2$, $|t| \Le T_0$ and $1 \Le m \Le T$, once
\begin{equation*}
T \Ge S \Ge 2 + \exp\bigl( (\log(T T_0))^{4/5} \bigr).
\end{equation*}
The condition $S \Le T$ holds, since $S \Le Z_2'$, and the hypothesis $U, V \Ge X^{\sigma}/C_1$ of Lemma~\ref{lem: type II}, \eqref{eq: parameters} and $\theta < 1/6$ give
\begin{equation*}
S \Le \frac{X}{UV} \Le C_1^2 X^{2\theta} \Le X^{\sigma} (\log X)^A = T.
\end{equation*} For the lower condition on $S$, the bounds $S \Ge Z_1' \Ge Z_1$, the hypothesis $Z_1 \Ge \mu^4/2$ of Lemma~\ref{lem: type II}, \eqref{eq: parameters}, $\log(T T_0) \Le 4 \log X$ and $4^{4/5} < 3.1$ give
\begin{equation*}
S \Ge \frac{\mu^4}{2} > 2 + \exp\bigl( (\log(T T_0))^{4/5} \bigr).
\end{equation*}
The function $S \exp(-\log S/(2(\log(T T_0))^{7/10}))$ increases with $S$, and $Z_2' \Le X/(UV)$. Since $\log Z_2' \Ge \log(\mu^4/2)$, $\log(T T_0) \Le 4 \log X$ and $4^{7/10} < 8/3$, once $X$ is large
\begin{equation*}
\frac{\log Z_2'}{2(\log(T T_0))^{7/10}} \Ge \frac{4(\log X)^{4/5} - \log 2}{2 \cdot 4^{7/10} (\log X)^{7/10}} \Ge \frac34 (\log X)^{1/10}.
\end{equation*}
So Lemma~\ref{lem: hecke pnt} gives, uniformly in $|t| \Le T_0$,
\begin{equation*}
\max_{m \Le T} |P_m(c + it)| \ll \frac{X}{UV} \exp\Bigl( -\frac34 (\log X)^{1/10} \Bigr).
\end{equation*}
Collecting the bounds in \eqref{eq: type II reduction}, we obtain
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{m \Le T} |E_m| \ll X (\log X)^{A + C + 1} \exp\Bigl( -\frac34 (\log X)^{1/10} \Bigr) + X^{\sigma + o(1)} \ll X (\log X)^{-A}.
\end{equation*}
\end{proof}

\section{Proofs of the sieve lemmas}
\label{app: sieve}

This appendix proves Lemmas~\ref{lem: fundamental}, \ref{lem: level large} and~\ref{lem: level small} of Section~\ref{sec: estimates} and Lemma~\ref{lem: decoupling} of Section~\ref{sec: decoupling}. In the proofs of Lemmas~\ref{lem: level large} and~\ref{lem: level small}, Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental} treats the sifting by the primes of norm below $\mu^4$, and Lemma~\ref{lem: type II} is applied only to primes of norm at least $\mu^4/2$. As in Section~\ref{sec: decoupling}, short intervals remove the conditions that couple the variables.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental}]
The proof is Brun's pure sieve: the inclusion--exclusion over the primes of norm below $z_0(u)$ that divide the cofactor is truncated after the divisors with $M$ prime factors, and the divisors with $M + 1$ prime factors bound the error of the truncation. Here $M := \lfloor (\varepsilon_0/4) (\log X)^{1/5} \rfloor - 1$. Once $X$ is large,
\begin{equation*}
\frac{\varepsilon_0}{5} (\log X)^{1/5} \Le M + 1 \Le \frac{\varepsilon_0}{4} (\log X)^{1/5},
\end{equation*}
so that $\mu^{4(M+1)} \Le X^{\varepsilon_0}$ by \eqref{eq: parameters} and $M + 1 \Ge e^2 \log\log X$. For $z \Ge 1$, let $\mathcal{D}^{+}(z)$ and $\mathcal{D}^{-}(z)$ be the sets of products of an even and of an odd number, at most $M$, of distinct primes of norm less than $z$, with the empty product $1$ in $\mathcal{D}^{+}(z)$, and let $\mathcal{D}^{0}(z)$ be the set of products of $M + 1$ distinct primes of norm less than $z$. We first show that $\sum_u c_u S'(\mathcal{E}_u, z_0(u))$ has an asymptotic formula if, for each $\mathcal{D} \in \{\mathcal{D}^+, \mathcal{D}^-, \mathcal{D}^0\}$, the sum of $c_u |\mathcal{E}_{uD}|$ over $u$ and over $D \in \mathcal{D}(z_0(u))$ has one.

For $u \mid n$, let $k$ be the number of distinct primes of norm less than $z_0(u)$ that divide $n/u$. Exactly $\binom{k}{i}$ products of $i$ distinct primes of norm less than $z_0(u)$ divide $n/u$. So every $n \in \mathcal{E}_u$ satisfies
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
\#\{D \in \mathcal{D}^+(z_0(u)) : D \mid n/u\} - \#\{D \in \mathcal{D}^-(z_0(u)) : D \mid n/u\} &= \sum_{i=0}^{M} (-1)^i \binom{k}{i}, \\
\#\{D \in \mathcal{D}^0(z_0(u)) : D \mid n/u\} &= \binom{k}{M+1}.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
The alternating sum is $1$ for $k = 0$, that is, for the $n$ counted by $S'(\mathcal{E}_u, z_0(u))$. For $k \Ge 1$,
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{i=0}^{M} (-1)^i \binom{k}{i} = (-1)^M \binom{k - 1}{M},
\end{equation*}
and this is at most $\binom{k}{M+1}$ in absolute value, since $\binom{k}{M+1} = \frac{k}{M+1} \binom{k - 1}{M}$ and $\binom{k - 1}{M} = 0$ for $k \Le M$. Summing over $n \in \mathcal{E}_u$, we obtain
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: bonferroni}
S'(\mathcal{E}_u, z_0(u)) = \sum_{D \in \mathcal{D}^+(z_0(u))} |\mathcal{E}_{uD}| - \sum_{D \in \mathcal{D}^-(z_0(u))} |\mathcal{E}_{uD}| + \Delta_u(\mathcal{E}), \qquad |\Delta_u(\mathcal{E})| \Le \sum_{D \in \mathcal{D}^0(z_0(u))} |\mathcal{E}_{uD}|.
\end{equation}

Multiply \eqref{eq: bonferroni} by $c_u$ and sum over $u$. By assumption, the sums of $c_u |\mathcal{A}_{uD}|$ over $u$ and over $D \in \mathcal{D}^{\pm}(z_0(u))$ are $2/(\pi X^{\sigma})$ times those for $\mathcal{B}$, up to $\mathcal{O}(X^{\tau}(\log X)^{-A})$. So
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
&\sum_u c_u S'(\mathcal{A}_u, z_0(u)) - \frac{2}{\pi X^{\sigma}} \sum_u c_u S'(\mathcal{B}_u, z_0(u)) \\
&\qquad = \sum_u c_u \Bigl( \Delta_u(\mathcal{A}) - \frac{2}{\pi X^{\sigma}}\, \Delta_u(\mathcal{B}) \Bigr) + \mathcal{O}\bigl(X^{\tau}(\log X)^{-A}\bigr).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation*}
The bound for $|\Delta_u(\mathcal{E})|$ in \eqref{eq: bonferroni} and the assumption for $\mathcal{D}^0$ give
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
\Bigl| \sum_u c_u \Bigl( \Delta_u(\mathcal{A}) - \frac{2}{\pi X^{\sigma}}\, \Delta_u(\mathcal{B}) \Bigr) \Bigr| &\Le \sum_u c_u \sum_{D \in \mathcal{D}^0(z_0(u))} \Bigl( |\mathcal{A}_{uD}| + \frac{2}{\pi X^{\sigma}} |\mathcal{B}_{uD}| \Bigr) \\
&= \frac{4}{\pi X^{\sigma}} \sum_u c_u \sum_{D \in \mathcal{D}^0(z_0(u))} |\mathcal{B}_{uD}| + \mathcal{O}\bigl(X^{\tau}(\log X)^{-A}\bigr).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation*}

Every $D \in \mathcal{D}^0(z_0(u))$ is a product of $M + 1$ distinct primes of norm below $\mu^4$, and, since $\log\log \mu^4 = (4/5) \log\log X + \log 4$ by \eqref{eq: parameters}, Lemma~\ref{lem: prime ideal theorem}(b) gives $\sum_{|\varpi|^2 < \mu^4} |\varpi|^{-2} \Le \log\log X$ once $X$ is large, so
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{D \in \mathcal{D}^0(z_0(u))} |D|^{-2} \Le \frac{(\log\log X)^{M+1}}{(M+1)!}.
\end{equation*} By Lemma~\ref{lem: counting}(a) and~(b),
\begin{equation*}
\sum_u c_u \sum_{D \in \mathcal{D}^0(z_0(u))} |\mathcal{B}_{uD}| \Le C X \sum_u c_u |u|^{-2} \frac{(\log\log X)^{M+1}}{(M+1)!} \ll X (\log X)^C \Bigl( \frac{e \log\log X}{M + 1} \Bigr)^{M+1} \Le X (\log X)^C e^{-(M+1)},
\end{equation*}
since $M + 1 \Ge e^2 \log\log X$. Finally $e^{-(M+1)} \Le \exp(-(\varepsilon_0/5)(\log X)^{1/5})$ is smaller than every power of $1/\log X$. Hence
\begin{equation*}
\sum_u c_u S'(\mathcal{A}_u, z_0(u)) = \frac{2}{\pi X^{\sigma}} \sum_u c_u S'(\mathcal{B}_u, z_0(u)) + \mathcal{O}\bigl(X^{\tau}(\log X)^{-A}\bigr).
\end{equation*}

It remains to show that the assumption holds in cases (a) and (b). Every $D \in \mathcal{D}(z_0(u))$ has $|D|^2 < \mu^{4(M+1)} \Le X^{\varepsilon_0}$. In case (a), the coefficient $\sum_{v = uD,\ D \in \mathcal{D}(z_0(u))} c_u$ of $v$ is non-negative, at most $d(v)^{C_0 + 1}$, and supported on $|v|^2 \Le X^{\tau} \mu^{-1}$, so Lemma~\ref{lem: type I}(a) applies. In case (b), $z_0$ is constant. The products $u' = u_2 D$ with $b_{u_2} \neq 0$ and $D \in \mathcal{D}(z_0)$ have $|u'|^2 < 2U_2 X^{\varepsilon_0}$, and the coefficient $\sum_{u' = u_2 D,\ D \in \mathcal{D}(z_0)} b_{u_2}$ of $u'$ is at most $d(u')^{C_0 + 1}$. So $u'$ lies in one of the $\mathcal{O}(\log X)$ dyadic ranges $u' \sim U'$ with $U'$ among $U_2 X^{\varepsilon_0}, U_2 X^{\varepsilon_0}/2, U_2 X^{\varepsilon_0}/4, \dots$. On each of them, the bounds on $U_2$ and $U_1 U_2$ in (b) give $U' \Le X^{\rho} \mu^{-1}$ and $U_1 U' \Le X^{\nu} \mu^{-1}$, and Lemma~\ref{lem: type I}(b) applies with $u_1$ and $u'$ as its variables $u$ and $u'$.
\end{proof}

The proofs of Lemmas~\ref{lem: level large} and~\ref{lem: level small} confine primes to the short intervals $J_\lambda$ of \eqref{eq: short intervals}, with the indices $\lambda_q$ defined after it. The following estimates bound the terms in which the norm of a prime lies in the short interval of a level or of another prime. For an index $\lambda$ put
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: short interval sum}
w_\lambda := \sum_{|\varpi|^2 \in J_\lambda} |\varpi|^{-2}.
\end{equation}
If $\xi_{\lambda + 1} > \mu^4$, then \eqref{eq: short intervals} gives $\xi_\lambda \Ge \mu^4/2 \Ge \mu$, so Lemma~\ref{lem: prime ideal theorem}(a) gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: short interval mass}
w_\lambda \Le \frac{2}{H \log(\mu^4/2)}.
\end{equation}
In sums over $\lambda$, the index runs over the $\lambda$ with $J_\lambda \cap [1, 2X] \neq \emptyset$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: prime ideal theorem}(b), $\sum_\lambda w_\lambda \Le 2\log\log X$, so \eqref{eq: short interval mass} gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: short interval square}
\sum_{\lambda:\ \xi_{\lambda + 1} > \mu^4} w_\lambda^2 \Le \frac{4 \log\log X}{H \log(\mu^4/2)}.
\end{equation}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: level large}]
Buchstab's identity \eqref{eq: buchstab} lowers the level $l(n)$ to $\mu^4$, where Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental} applies. Fix $A$, let $C$ be such that $\sum_{|n|^2 \Le X} a_n |n|^{-2} \Le (\log X)^C$ (Lemma~\ref{lem: counting}(b)), and take $B = A + C + 3$. Split the support of $(a_n)$ into $\mathcal{N}_1 := \{n : l(n) \Le \mu^4\}$ and $\mathcal{N}_2 := \{n : l(n) > \mu^4\}$. On $\mathcal{N}_1$, Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental}(a) applies with $c_n = a_n \mathbf{1}[n \in \mathcal{N}_1]$, the level $z_0 = l$ and $\varepsilon_0 = \varepsilon/2$, since $X^{\tau - \varepsilon} \Le X^{\tau - \varepsilon/2} \mu^{-1}$. On $\mathcal{N}_2$, Buchstab's identity \eqref{eq: buchstab} gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: buchstab at mu4}
S'(\mathcal{E}_n, l(n)) = S'(\mathcal{E}_n, \mu^4) - \sum_{\mu^4 \Le |q|^2 < l(n)} S'(\mathcal{E}_{nq}, q),
\end{equation}
and the term $S'(\mathcal{E}_n, \mu^4)$ of \eqref{eq: buchstab at mu4}, summed with $a_n$, has an asymptotic formula by Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental}(a) with the constant level $\mu^4$ and $\varepsilon_0 = \varepsilon/2$. It remains to treat
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: sigma zero}
\Sigma_0(\mathcal{E}) := \sum_{n \in \mathcal{N}_2} a_n \sum_{\mu^4 \Le |q|^2 < l(n)} S'(\mathcal{E}_{nq}, q).
\end{equation}

Two conditions couple $n$, $q$ and the cofactor: $|q|^2 < l(n)$, and the condition that every prime factor of the cofactor is $\succcurlyeq q$. For $n \in \mathcal{N}_2$ let $\lambda(n)$ be the index with $l(n) \in J_{\lambda(n)}$. Then
\begin{equation*}
\mathbf{1}[|q|^2 < l(n)] = \mathbf{1}[\lambda_q < \lambda(n)] + \mathbf{1}[\lambda_q = \lambda(n)]\, \mathbf{1}[|q|^2 < l(n)].
\end{equation*}
Moreover, a prime factor $\varpi' \prec q$ of the cofactor with norm at least $\xi_{\lambda_q}$ has its norm in $J_{\lambda_q}$, so
\begin{equation*}
0 \Le S'(\mathcal{E}_{nq}, \xi_{\lambda_q}) - S'(\mathcal{E}_{nq}, q) \Le \sum_{|\varpi'|^2 \in J_{\lambda_q}} |\mathcal{E}_{nq\varpi'}|.
\end{equation*}
By the splitting of $\mathbf{1}[|q|^2 < l(n)]$, $\Sigma_0(\mathcal{E})$ consists of its terms with $\lambda_q < \lambda(n)$ and its terms with $\lambda_q = \lambda(n)$. In each term with $\lambda_q < \lambda(n)$, the bound for $S'(\mathcal{E}_{nq}, \xi_{\lambda_q}) - S'(\mathcal{E}_{nq}, q)$ replaces $S'(\mathcal{E}_{nq}, q)$ by $S'(\mathcal{E}_{nq}, \xi_{\lambda_q})$ with an error of at most $a_n \sum_{|\varpi'|^2 \in J_{\lambda_q}} |\mathcal{E}_{nq\varpi'}|$. Each term with $\lambda_q = \lambda(n)$ lies between $0$ and $a_n |\mathcal{E}_{nq}|$, since $S'(\mathcal{E}_{nq}, q) \Le |\mathcal{E}_{nq}|$. Grouped by $\lambda = \lambda_q$, these bounds give
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: level large split}
\Bigl| \Sigma_0(\mathcal{E}) - \sum_\lambda M_\lambda(\mathcal{E}) \Bigr| \Le \sum_\lambda \bigl( R_\lambda(\mathcal{E}) + R'_\lambda(\mathcal{E}) \bigr),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation*}
\begin{aligned}
M_\lambda(\mathcal{E}) &:= \sum_{\lambda(n) > \lambda} a_n \sum_{|q|^2 \in J_\lambda,\ |q|^2 \Ge \mu^4} S'(\mathcal{E}_{nq}, \xi_\lambda), \\
R_\lambda(\mathcal{E}) &:= \sum_{\lambda(n) = \lambda} a_n \sum_{|q|^2 \in J_\lambda,\ |q|^2 \Ge \mu^4} |\mathcal{E}_{nq}|, \\
R'_\lambda(\mathcal{E}) &:= \sum_{\lambda(n) \Ge \lambda} a_n \sum_{|q|^2 \in J_\lambda,\ |q|^2 \Ge \mu^4}\ \sum_{|\varpi'|^2 \in J_\lambda} |\mathcal{E}_{nq\varpi'}|.
\end{aligned}
\end{equation*}

Each of these sums has the form
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{n, v} a_n b_v\, \#\{q : |q|^2 \in J_\lambda,\ |q|^2 \Ge \mu^4,\ nvq \in \mathcal{E}\},
\end{equation*}
with $n$ restricted to $\lambda(n) > \lambda$, $\lambda(n) = \lambda$ or $\lambda(n) \Ge \lambda$, and with $v$ the cofactor of $nq$. For $M_\lambda$, the coefficient $b_v$ is the indicator that every prime factor of $v$ has norm at least $\xi_\lambda$. For $R_\lambda$ it is $1$, and for $R'_\lambda$ it is the number of primes of norm in $J_\lambda$ dividing $v$, which is at most $d(v)$. If $\lambda(n) \Ge \lambda$ and $|q|^2 \in J_\lambda$, then
\begin{equation*}
|q|^2 < \xi_{\lambda+1} \Le (1 + 1/H)\, l(n) \Le \frac{2C_l X^{\tau}}{|n|^2},
\end{equation*}
so \eqref{eq: sets} and \eqref{eq: parameters} give
\begin{equation*}
|v|^2 \Ge \frac{X/2}{|nq|^2} > \frac{X/2}{2C_l X^{\tau}} = \frac{X^{\sigma}}{4C_l}.
\end{equation*} In addition, $|n|^2 \Ge X^{\sigma}/C_l$ and $|q|^2 \Ge \mu^4$. After $n$ and $v$ are split into dyadic ranges $n \sim U$ and $v \sim V$, which have $U \Ge X^{\sigma}/(2C_l)$ and $V \Ge X^{\sigma}/(8C_l)$, Lemma~\ref{lem: type II} with $n$ as its variable $u$, $C_1 = 8C_l$, $Z_1 = \max(\xi_\lambda, \mu^4)$ and $Z_2 = \xi_{\lambda+1}$ gives $M_\lambda$, $R_\lambda$ and $R'_\lambda$ asymptotic formulas, uniformly in $\lambda$. Here $Z_1 < Z_2$ for the $\lambda$ with $\xi_{\lambda+1} > \mu^4$, and for the other $\lambda$ these sums vanish. At most $2H \log X$ indices and $\mathcal{O}((\log X)^2)$ pairs of dyadic ranges occur, so Lemma~\ref{lem: type II} is applied $\mathcal{O}(H (\log X)^3)$ times. With $A + B + 5$ in place of $A$ in each application, the errors add up to $\mathcal{O}(X^{\tau}(\log X)^{-A})$.

By \eqref{eq: level large split} and the asymptotic formulas for the sums $M_\lambda$,
\begin{equation*}
\Sigma_0(\mathcal{A}) - \frac{2}{\pi X^{\sigma}} \Sigma_0(\mathcal{B}) \ll \sum_\lambda (R_\lambda + R'_\lambda)(\mathcal{A}) + \frac{2}{\pi X^{\sigma}} \sum_\lambda (R_\lambda + R'_\lambda)(\mathcal{B}) + X^{\tau}(\log X)^{-A},
\end{equation*}
and the sums over $\mathcal{A}$ equal $\frac{2}{\pi X^{\sigma}}$ times those over $\mathcal{B}$ up to the same error. Lemma~\ref{lem: counting}(a) and \eqref{eq: short interval sum} give
\begin{equation*}
\sum_\lambda R_\lambda(\mathcal{B}) \ll X \sum_n a_n |n|^{-2} w_{\lambda(n)}, \qquad \sum_\lambda R'_\lambda(\mathcal{B}) \ll X \sum_n a_n |n|^{-2} \sum_{\lambda:\ \xi_{\lambda + 1} > \mu^4} w_\lambda^2,
\end{equation*}
since $\mu^4 \Le |q|^2 < \xi_{\lambda + 1}$ in $R'_\lambda$. For $n \in \mathcal{N}_2$, $\xi_{\lambda(n) + 1} > l(n) > \mu^4$. So, by \eqref{eq: short interval mass} and \eqref{eq: short interval square},
\begin{equation*}
\sum_\lambda R_\lambda(\mathcal{B}) + \sum_\lambda R'_\lambda(\mathcal{B}) \ll \frac{X (\log X)^C \log\log X}{H (\log X)^{4/5}} \ll X (\log X)^{-A}.
\end{equation*} Hence $\Sigma_0$ has an asymptotic formula, and so does $\sum_n a_n S'(\mathcal{E}_n, l(n))$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: level small}]
Buchstab's identity \eqref{eq: buchstab} is iterated until the product of $n$ and the primes it introduces has norm at least $X^{\sigma}$, and Lemma~\ref{lem: level large} is applied to that product. Fix $A$, let $C$ be as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: level large}, and take $B = A + C + 4$. With $\mathcal{N}_1$ and $\mathcal{N}_2$ as in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: level large}, Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental}(a) applies on $\mathcal{N}_1$ with the level $l$ and $\varepsilon_0 = \theta$, since \eqref{eq: parameters} gives
\begin{equation*}
|n|^2 < X^{\sigma} = X^{\tau - 2\theta} \Le X^{\tau - \theta} \mu^{-1}.
\end{equation*} On $\mathcal{N}_2$, \eqref{eq: buchstab at mu4} holds, and the term $S'(\mathcal{E}_n, \mu^4)$ of \eqref{eq: buchstab at mu4} again has an asymptotic formula by Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental}(a), with $\varepsilon_0 = \theta$. It remains to treat the sum $\Sigma_0(\mathcal{E})$ of \eqref{eq: sigma zero}.

A \emph{chain of length} $i \Ge 0$ is a tuple $\boldsymbol{p} = (n, p_1, \dots, p_i, q)$ with $n \in \mathcal{N}_2$ and primes $p_1 \succ \dots \succ p_i \succ q$ of norm at least $\mu^4$, such that the largest of these primes ($p_1$, or $q$ if $i = 0$) has norm less than $l(n)$, and $|n p_1 \cdots p_{i'}|^2 < X^{\sigma}$ for $1 \Le i' \Le i$. Write $N(\boldsymbol{p}) := n p_1 \cdots p_i q$ and $q(\boldsymbol{p}) := q$. Let $\mathcal{K}_i$ be the set of chains of length $i$, split into the exits $\mathcal{K}^{\Ge}_i$, with $|N(\boldsymbol{p})|^2 \Ge X^{\sigma}$, and the continuations $\mathcal{K}^{<}_i$, with $|N(\boldsymbol{p})|^2 < X^{\sigma}$, and put
\begin{equation*}
\Sigma_i(\mathcal{E}) := \sum_{\boldsymbol{p} \in \mathcal{K}_i} a_n S'(\mathcal{E}_{N(\boldsymbol{p})}, q(\boldsymbol{p})) \qquad (i \Ge 1).
\end{equation*}
The same sum over $\mathcal{K}_0$ is $\Sigma_0(\mathcal{E})$ of \eqref{eq: sigma zero}. For $\boldsymbol{p} \in \mathcal{K}^{<}_i$, Buchstab's identity \eqref{eq: buchstab} gives
\begin{equation*}
S'(\mathcal{E}_{N(\boldsymbol{p})}, q) = S'(\mathcal{E}_{N(\boldsymbol{p})}, \mu^4) - \sum_{q' \prec q,\ |q'|^2 \Ge \mu^4} S'(\mathcal{E}_{N(\boldsymbol{p}) q'}, q').
\end{equation*}
The map $(\boldsymbol{p}, q') \mapsto (n, p_1, \dots, p_i, q, q')$, on the pairs that occur in these sums, is a bijection onto $\mathcal{K}_{i+1}$. Hence $\Sigma_i = \Sigma^{\Ge}_i + F_i - \Sigma_{i+1}$, where
\begin{equation*}
\Sigma^{\Ge}_i(\mathcal{E}) := \sum_{\boldsymbol{p} \in \mathcal{K}^{\Ge}_i} a_n S'(\mathcal{E}_{N(\boldsymbol{p})}, q(\boldsymbol{p})), \qquad F_i(\mathcal{E}) := \sum_{\boldsymbol{p} \in \mathcal{K}^{<}_i} a_n S'(\mathcal{E}_{N(\boldsymbol{p})}, \mu^4).
\end{equation*}
A chain of length $i$ has $\mu^{4i} \Le |p_1 \cdots p_i|^2 < X^{\sigma}$, so $\mathcal{K}_i$ is empty for $i > i_{\max} := \lfloor \sigma (\log X)^{1/5}/4 \rfloor$. Hence, for $\mathcal{E} = \mathcal{A}$ and for $\mathcal{E} = \mathcal{B}$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: chain sum}
\Sigma_0 = \sum_{i=0}^{i_{\max}} (-1)^i (\Sigma^{\Ge}_i + F_i).
\end{equation}

The primes of a chain are distinct and their order is forced, so a chain is determined by its product and the set of its primes. The number of chains with a given product $N$ is therefore at most $d(N)$, and every coefficient defined from chains in this proof is at most $d(N)^{C_0 + 1}$, uniformly in $i$. The sum $F_i$ is $\sum_N c_i(N) S'(\mathcal{E}_N, \mu^4)$, where $c_i(N)$ is the sum of $a_n$ over the chains $\boldsymbol{p} \in \mathcal{K}^{<}_i$ with $N(\boldsymbol{p}) = N$. This $c_i(N)$ is supported on $|N|^2 < X^{\sigma}$, so Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental}(a) with $\varepsilon_0 = \theta$ gives $F_i$ an asymptotic formula, uniformly in $i$.

Let $C_E := 2 C_l^{\lceil \sigma/\theta \rceil + 2}$. We claim that every $\boldsymbol{p} \in \mathcal{K}^{\Ge}_i$ satisfies
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: exit bound}
X^{\sigma} \Le |N(\boldsymbol{p})|^2 \Le C_E X^{\tau - \theta/2}, \qquad |N(\boldsymbol{p})|^2 |q(\boldsymbol{p})|^2 \Le C_E X^{\tau}.
\end{equation}
Every exit has $|n p_1 \cdots p_i|^2 < X^{\sigma} \Le |N(\boldsymbol{p})|^2$, by the definition of a chain for $i \Ge 1$ and by $|n|^2 < X^{\sigma}$ for $i = 0$. Since $1 \Le |n|^2 < X^{\sigma}$, \eqref{eq: j function} and \eqref{eq: j interval} give $0 \Le j(|n|^2) < \sigma/\theta$. By \eqref{eq: j interval}, $|n|^2 \Ge X^{\sigma - (j(|n|^2)+1)\theta}$, so \eqref{eq: z function} and the relation $\tau = \sigma + 2\theta$ of \eqref{eq: parameters} give
\begin{equation*}
z(|n|^2) \Le \Bigl( \frac{X^{\tau}}{X^{\sigma - (j(|n|^2)+1)\theta}} \Bigr)^{1/(j(|n|^2)+2)} = X^{(j(|n|^2)+3)\theta/(j(|n|^2)+2)} \Le X^{3\theta/2}.
\end{equation*} The hypothesis $l(n) \Le C_l\, z(|n|^2)$ and \eqref{eq: z function} give
\begin{equation*}
|n|^2\, l(n)^{j(|n|^2)+2} \Le C_l^{j(|n|^2)+2} X^{\tau}.
\end{equation*}
If $|q|^2 \Le X^{\theta}$, then
\begin{equation*}
|N(\boldsymbol{p})|^2 |q|^2 = |n p_1 \cdots p_i|^2 |q|^4 < X^{\sigma + 2\theta} = X^{\tau}.
\end{equation*} If $|q|^2 > X^{\theta}$, then every prime of $\boldsymbol{p}$ has norm greater than $X^{\theta}$, so
\begin{equation*}
|n|^2 X^{i\theta} \Le |n p_1 \cdots p_i|^2 < X^{\sigma} \Le |n|^2 X^{(j(|n|^2)+1)\theta}.
\end{equation*}
This gives $i \Le j(|n|^2)$. Every prime of $\boldsymbol{p}$ has norm less than $l(n)$, so
\begin{equation*}
|N(\boldsymbol{p})|^2 |q|^2 < |n|^2\, l(n)^{i+2} \Le |n|^2\, l(n)^{j(|n|^2)+2} \Le C_l^{j(|n|^2)+2} X^{\tau}.
\end{equation*}
It remains to bound $|N(\boldsymbol{p})|^2$. Since $|n p_1 \cdots p_i|^2 < X^{\sigma}$ and $|q|^2 < l(n) \Le C_l X^{3\theta/2}$,
\begin{equation*}
|N(\boldsymbol{p})|^2 < X^{\sigma}|q|^2 < C_l X^{\sigma + 3\theta/2} = C_l X^{\tau - \theta/2}.
\end{equation*}

Now confine the prime $q(\boldsymbol{p})$ to short intervals. For $\boldsymbol{p} \in \mathcal{K}^{\Ge}_i$ with $\lambda_{q(\boldsymbol{p})} = \lambda$,
\begin{equation*}
0 \Le S'(\mathcal{E}_{N(\boldsymbol{p})}, \xi_\lambda) - S'(\mathcal{E}_{N(\boldsymbol{p})}, q(\boldsymbol{p})) \Le \sum_{|\varpi'|^2 \in J_\lambda} |\mathcal{E}_{N(\boldsymbol{p})\varpi'}|.
\end{equation*} Hence
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: exit split}
\Bigl| \Sigma^{\Ge}_i(\mathcal{E}) - \sum_\lambda \sum_N c_{i,\lambda}(N) S'(\mathcal{E}_N, \xi_\lambda) \Bigr| \Le R_i(\mathcal{E}), \qquad R_i(\mathcal{E}) := \sum_\lambda \sum_N c_{i,\lambda}(N) \sum_{|\varpi'|^2 \in J_\lambda} |\mathcal{E}_{N\varpi'}|,
\end{equation}
where $c_{i,\lambda}(N)$ is the sum of $a_n$ over the chains $\boldsymbol{p} \in \mathcal{K}^{\Ge}_i$ with $N(\boldsymbol{p}) = N$ and $\lambda_{q(\boldsymbol{p})} = \lambda$. By \eqref{eq: exit bound}, every $N$ in the support of $c_{i,\lambda}$ and every chain $\boldsymbol{p}$ counted in $c_{i,\lambda}(N)$ satisfy
\begin{equation*}
X^{\sigma} \Le |N|^2 \Le C_E X^{\tau - \theta/2} \Le X^{\tau - \theta/4}, \qquad \xi_\lambda \Le |q(\boldsymbol{p})|^2 \Le C_E X^{\tau}/|N|^2.
\end{equation*} So Lemma~\ref{lem: level large}, with the constant level $\xi_\lambda$, with $\theta/4$ in place of $\varepsilon$ and with $C_E$ in place of $C_l$, gives each sum $\sum_N c_{i,\lambda}(N) S'(\mathcal{E}_N, \xi_\lambda)$ in \eqref{eq: exit split} an asymptotic formula.

For each $\lambda$, the sum over $N$ and $\varpi'$ in $R_i$ is a sum \eqref{eq: type II} with $N$ as its variable $u$, $\varpi'$ as its prime $\varpi$ and the cofactor $v$ of $N\varpi'$ as its variable $v$. The norm of $\varpi'$ lies in $J_\lambda$, and $\xi_\lambda \Ge \mu^4/2$ since $\xi_{\lambda+1} > |q(\boldsymbol{p})|^2 \Ge \mu^4$. Since $\xi_{\lambda+1} = (1 + 1/H)\, \xi_\lambda$ by \eqref{eq: short intervals}, $\xi_\lambda |N|^2 \Le C_E X^{\tau}$ and $\sigma + \tau = 1$ by \eqref{eq: parameters}, the cofactor has
\begin{equation*}
|v|^2 > \frac{X/2}{|N|^2\, \xi_{\lambda+1}} \Ge \frac{X/2}{(1 + 1/H)\, C_E X^{\tau}} \Ge \frac{X^{\sigma}}{3C_E}.
\end{equation*} After $N$ and $v$ are split into dyadic ranges, Lemma~\ref{lem: type II} with $C_1 = 6C_E$, $Z_1 = \xi_\lambda$ and $Z_2 = \xi_{\lambda+1}$ gives this sum an asymptotic formula, uniformly in $\lambda$.

The \emph{stage} $i$ consists of the sums $\Sigma^{\Ge}_i$ and $F_i$. Each stage has at most $2H \log X$ indices $\lambda$, at most $\log X$ dyadic ranges of $N$ and at most $\log X$ dyadic ranges of $v$, since $X^{\sigma} \Le |N|^2 \Le X^{\tau - \theta/4}$ and $X^{\sigma}/(3C_E) \Le |v|^2 < X^{\tau}$. So \eqref{eq: chain sum} and \eqref{eq: exit split} express $\Sigma_0$ through at most $2H(\log X)^2$ applications of Lemma~\ref{lem: level large}, at most $2H(\log X)^3$ of Lemma~\ref{lem: type II} and $1$ of Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental} per stage, over at most $(\log X)^{1/5}$ stages. These are $\mathcal{O}(H (\log X)^{16/5})$ sums in all. With $A + B + 5$ in place of $A$ in each, their errors add up to $\mathcal{O}(X^{\tau}(\log X)^{-A})$.

It remains to bound the remainders $R_i$. By Lemma~\ref{lem: counting}(a) and \eqref{eq: short interval sum},
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{i=0}^{i_{\max}} R_i(\mathcal{B}) \ll X \sum_n a_n |n|^{-2} \sum_{i \Ge 0} \sum_{p_1 \succ \dots \succ p_i} \prod_{h=1}^{i} |p_h|^{-2} \sum_{\lambda:\ \xi_{\lambda + 1} > \mu^4} w_\lambda^2,
\end{equation*}
where the factor $w_\lambda^2$ comes from the primes $q$ and $\varpi'$, whose norms both lie in $J_\lambda$, and $|q|^2 \Ge \mu^4$ gives $\xi_{\lambda + 1} > \mu^4$. The sum of $\prod_{h=1}^{i} |p_h|^{-2}$ over the tuples $p_1 \succ \dots \succ p_i$ of primes of norm at most $X$ is at most $(2\log\log X)^i/i!$ by Lemma~\ref{lem: prime ideal theorem}(b), so the sum over $i$ is at most $(\log X)^2$. With \eqref{eq: short interval square},
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{i=0}^{i_{\max}} R_i(\mathcal{B}) \ll \frac{X (\log X)^{C+2} \log\log X}{H (\log X)^{4/5}} \ll X(\log X)^{-A},
\end{equation*}
and the asymptotic formulas for the sums $R_i$ give
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{i=0}^{i_{\max}} R_i(\mathcal{A}) \ll X^{\tau}(\log X)^{-A}.
\end{equation*} Summing \eqref{eq: chain sum} term by term gives
\begin{equation*}
\Sigma_0(\mathcal{A}) - \frac{2}{\pi X^{\sigma}}\, \Sigma_0(\mathcal{B}) = \mathcal{O}\bigl(X^{\tau}(\log X)^{-A}\bigr).
\end{equation*}
Hence $\Sigma_0$ has an asymptotic formula, and so does $\sum_n a_n S'(\mathcal{E}_n, l(n))$.
\end{proof}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: decoupling}]
The proof compares $\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}$, tuple by tuple, with the sum of the pieces $\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\mathcal{E}; y_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}})$ of \eqref{eq: pieces} over the index vectors that satisfy all constraints. Fix $A$ and take $B = A + 1$. For a tuple $\boldsymbol{\varpi}$ with $|\varpi_i|^2 \in \mathcal{I}_i$ for $1 \Le i \Le k$, let $\boldsymbol{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{\varpi}) := (\lambda_{\varpi_1}, \dots, \lambda_{\varpi_k})$ be its index vector, so that $\boldsymbol{\varpi} \in \mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{\varpi}))$. Let $g_c(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) := 1$ if $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$ satisfies a constraint $c$ and $h_c(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) := 1$ if $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$ straddles $c$, and let both be $0$ otherwise. An index vector that neither satisfies nor straddles $c$ has no tuple satisfying $c$, so
\begin{equation*}
\mathbf{1}_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi}) = g_c(\boldsymbol{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{\varpi})) + \mathbf{1}_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi})\, h_c(\boldsymbol{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{\varpi})), \qquad g_c h_c = 0.
\end{equation*}

Let $\Xi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(t)$ be the indicator that every prime factor of $t$ has norm at least the level $y_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}$ of \eqref{eq: piece level}. For $\boldsymbol{\lambda} = \boldsymbol{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{\varpi})$ we have $\Xi(\boldsymbol{\varpi}, t) \Le \Xi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(t)$, and, by the paragraph after \eqref{eq: window floor}, the difference is at most the number of prime factors of $t$ with norm in $J_{\lambda'}$ for some $\lambda'$ in the window $\mathcal{W}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$ of \eqref{eq: window}. All these indicators take values in $\{0, 1\}$. If $\prod_c g_c(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) = 1$, then $h_c(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) = 0$ and $\mathbf{1}_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi}) = 1$ for every $c$. Otherwise $g_c(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) = 0$ for some $c$, and $\prod_c \mathbf{1}_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi}) = 1$ forces $h_c(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) = 1$ for this $c$, by $\mathbf{1}_c = g_c + \mathbf{1}_c h_c$. So, for $\boldsymbol{\lambda} = \boldsymbol{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{\varpi})$,
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: decoupling pointwise}
\Bigl| \prod_c \mathbf{1}_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi})\, \Xi(\boldsymbol{\varpi}, t) - \prod_c g_c(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\, \Xi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(t) \Bigr| \Le \sum_c h_c(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) + \prod_c g_c(\boldsymbol{\lambda})\, \bigl(\Xi_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(t) - \Xi(\boldsymbol{\varpi}, t)\bigr).
\end{equation}

Let $\mathcal{L}_0$ be the set of index vectors that satisfy all constraints, and $\mathcal{L}_c$ the set of those that straddle $c$, so that $\prod_c g_c$ is the indicator of $\mathcal{L}_0$ and $h_c$ that of $\mathcal{L}_c$. Multiply \eqref{eq: decoupling pointwise} by the indicator that $\varpi_1 \cdots \varpi_k t \in \mathcal{E}$ and $|\varpi_i|^2 \in \mathcal{I}_i$ for $1 \Le i \Le k$, and sum over the tuples $\boldsymbol{\varpi}$ and over $t$. By \eqref{eq: configuration sum} and \eqref{eq: pieces}, this gives
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: decoupling sum}
\Bigl| \Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{E}) - \sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda} \in \mathcal{L}_0} \mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\mathcal{E}; y_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}) \Bigr| \Le E^+(\mathcal{E}),
\end{equation}
where
\begin{equation*}
E^+(\mathcal{E}) := \sum_c \sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda} \in \mathcal{L}_c} \mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\mathcal{E}; 1) + \sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda} \in \mathcal{L}_0} \sum_{\lambda' \in \mathcal{W}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})} \mathcal{P}'_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\lambda'}(\mathcal{E}).
\end{equation*}
Since $B = A + 1$, at most $(2H \log X)^k = 2^k (\log X)^{k(A+2)}$ index vectors have $\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda}) \neq \emptyset$ by \eqref{eq: short intervals}, and each window \eqref{eq: window} has at most $2k + 2$ elements. Then \eqref{eq: decoupling sum} for $\mathcal{A}$ and for $\mathcal{B}$, and the hypotheses with $A + (k+1)(A+3)$ in place of $A$, give
\begin{align*}
\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{A}) - \frac{2}{\pi X^{\sigma}}\Sigma_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{B}) &= \mathcal{O}\Bigl(E^+(\mathcal{A}) + \frac{2}{\pi X^{\sigma}} E^+(\mathcal{B})\Bigr) + \mathcal{O}\bigl(X^{\tau}(\log X)^{-A}\bigr), \\
E^+(\mathcal{A}) &= \frac{2}{\pi X^{\sigma}} E^+(\mathcal{B}) + \mathcal{O}\bigl(X^{\tau}(\log X)^{-A}\bigr).
\end{align*}
Since $H = (\log X)^{A+1}$ and $(\log\log X)^k \Le (\log X)^{4/5}$ once $X$ is large, it suffices to show
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: diagonal bound}
E^+(\mathcal{B}) \ll \frac{X (\log\log X)^k}{H (\log X)^{4/5}}.
\end{equation}

By Lemma~\ref{lem: counting}(a), a tuple $\boldsymbol{\varpi}$ contributes at most $CX/|\varpi_1 \cdots \varpi_k|^2$ to $\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\mathcal{B}; 1)$, and a tuple $(\boldsymbol{\varpi}, \varpi')$ at most $CX/|\varpi_1 \cdots \varpi_k \varpi'|^2$ to $\mathcal{P}'_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\lambda'}(\mathcal{B})$. If $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$ straddles $c$, then $\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$ contains tuples with $m_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi}) \Le Z_c$ and tuples with $m_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi}) \Ge Z_c$. Since each $|\varpi_i|^2$ varies by at most the factor $1 + 1/H$ on $\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$ and $|e_{c,i}| \Le 2$, $m_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi})$ varies there by at most the factor $(1 + 1/H)^{2k}$. So every tuple of $\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$ has $m_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi})$ within the factor $(1 + 1/H)^{2k}$ of $Z_c$. The sets $\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$ are disjoint, so the sum of $\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\mathcal{B}; 1)$ over $\boldsymbol{\lambda} \in \mathcal{L}_c$ runs over distinct tuples, each with $m_c(\boldsymbol{\varpi})$ within the factor $(1 + 1/H)^{2k}$ of $Z_c$. Choose $i_1$ with $e_{c,i_1} \neq 0$. For fixed $\varpi_i$, $i \neq i_1$, the factor $(1 + 1/H)^{2k}$ on each side of $Z_c$ and $|e_{c,i_1}| \Ge 1$ confine the norm $|\varpi_{i_1}|^2$ to an interval $[Y, Y(1 + 1/H)^{4k})$, with $Y \Ge \mu^4$ since $\mathcal{I}_{i_1} \subset [\mu^4, X]$, and Lemma~\ref{lem: prime ideal theorem}(a) bounds the sum of $|\varpi_{i_1}|^{-2}$ over it by $8k/(H \log \mu^4)$. Each other variable contributes at most $\sum_{|\varpi|^2 \Le X} |\varpi|^{-2} \Le 2\log\log X$, by Lemma~\ref{lem: prime ideal theorem}(b). Since $\log \mu^4 = 4(\log X)^{4/5}$ by \eqref{eq: parameters}, the sums over $\mathcal{L}_c$ in $E^+(\mathcal{B})$ satisfy
\begin{equation*}
\sum_c \sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda} \in \mathcal{L}_c} \mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}(\mathcal{B}; 1) \Le C X \sum_c \frac{8k\, (2\log\log X)^{k-1}}{H \log \mu^4} \ll \frac{X (\log\log X)^k}{H (\log X)^{4/5}}.
\end{equation*}

In the sum over $\mathcal{L}_0$, for fixed $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$, the norm of $\varpi'$ runs over an interval $[Y', Y'(1 + 1/H)^{2k+2})$ with $Y' \Ge \mu^4/2$ by \eqref{eq: window floor}, and Lemma~\ref{lem: prime ideal theorem}(a) bounds the sum of $|\varpi'|^{-2}$ over it by $(4k + 4)/(H \log(\mu^4/2))$. The remaining sums over $\mathcal{T}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})$ add up, over all $\boldsymbol{\lambda}$, to at most $(2\log\log X)^k$. So
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{\boldsymbol{\lambda} \in \mathcal{L}_0} \sum_{\lambda' \in \mathcal{W}(\boldsymbol{\lambda})} \mathcal{P}'_{\boldsymbol{\lambda},\lambda'}(\mathcal{B}) \Le C X\, \frac{4k + 4}{H \log(\mu^4/2)}\, (2\log\log X)^k \ll \frac{X (\log\log X)^k}{H (\log X)^{4/5}},
\end{equation*}
and with the bound for the sums over $\mathcal{L}_c$ this gives \eqref{eq: diagonal bound}.
\end{proof}

\section{Discarded sums as integrals}
\label{app: integrals}

This appendix proves Lemma~\ref{lem: boxes to integrals}, which the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: lower bound} uses to bound the discarded sums over $\mathcal{B}$.

\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: boxes to integrals}]
We first count tuples $(\varpi_0, \dots, \varpi_{K'})$ with a \emph{free prime} $\varpi_0$, restricted only by $\varpi_0 \cdots \varpi_{K'} \in \mathcal{B}$. Let $K' \Ge 1$, and let $\mathcal{R}' \subset \mathbb{R}^{K'}$ be bounded, with boundary of measure $0$, and with $y_i \Ge c_1$ and $\sum_{i=1}^{K'} y_i \Le 1 - c_1$ on $\mathcal{R}'$. We claim
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: prime tuples}
\#\bigl\{ (\varpi_0, \dots, \varpi_{K'}) : \boldsymbol{y}(\varpi_1, \dots, \varpi_{K'}) \in \mathcal{R}',\ \varpi_0 \cdots \varpi_{K'} \in \mathcal{B} \bigr\} = \frac{X}{2\log X} \Bigl( \int_{\mathcal{R}'} \frac{d\boldsymbol{y}}{y_1 \cdots y_{K'} (1 - \sum_{i=1}^{K'} y_i)} + o(1) \Bigr).
\end{equation}
For fixed $\varpi_1, \dots, \varpi_{K'}$ with $\prod_{i=1}^{K'} |\varpi_i|^2 \Le X^{1 - c_1}$, Lemma~\ref{lem: prime ideal theorem}(d) gives the number of $\varpi_0$ as
\begin{equation*}
\pi_G\Bigl( \frac{X}{\prod_{i=1}^{K'} |\varpi_i|^2} \Bigr) - \pi_G\Bigl( \frac{X}{2\prod_{i=1}^{K'} |\varpi_i|^2} \Bigr) = (1 + o(1)) \frac{X}{2 \prod_{i=1}^{K'} |\varpi_i|^2\, \log\bigl(X/\prod_{i=1}^{K'} |\varpi_i|^2\bigr)}.
\end{equation*}
So the number of tuples in \eqref{eq: prime tuples} is
\begin{equation*}
(1 + o(1)) \frac{X}{2\log X} \sum_{\varpi_1, \dots, \varpi_{K'}} F(\boldsymbol{y}(\varpi_1, \dots, \varpi_{K'})) \prod_{i=1}^{K'} |\varpi_i|^{-2}, \qquad F(\boldsymbol{y}) := \frac{\mathbf{1}[\boldsymbol{y} \in \mathcal{R}']}{1 - \sum_{i=1}^{K'} y_i}.
\end{equation*}
Let $m_X$ be the measure on $[c_1, 1]$ that gives mass $|\varpi|^{-2}$ to the point $\log|\varpi|^2/\log X$ for each prime $\varpi$ with $X^{c_1} \Le |\varpi|^2 \Le X$, so that
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{\varpi_1, \dots, \varpi_{K'}} F(\boldsymbol{y}(\varpi_1, \dots, \varpi_{K'})) \prod_{i=1}^{K'} |\varpi_i|^{-2} = \int F\, dm_X^{\otimes K'}.
\end{equation*} By Lemma~\ref{lem: prime ideal theorem}(c) with $a_0 = c_1$, the $m_X^{\otimes K'}$-measure of every product of intervals $\prod_{i=1}^{K'} [a_i, b_i)$ with $c_1 \Le a_i < b_i \Le 1$ tends to $\prod_{i=1}^{K'} \log(b_i/a_i)$, its mass under $\prod_{i=1}^{K'} dy_i/y_i$. The function $F$ is bounded and continuous outside the null set $\partial\mathcal{R}'$, hence Riemann integrable: there are step functions $F_- \Le F \Le F_+$ on finite grids of such products with $\int (F_+ - F_-) \prod_{i=1}^{K'} dy_i/y_i$ arbitrarily small. The integrals of $F_-$ and $F_+$ against $m_X^{\otimes K'}$ tend to their integrals against $\prod_{i=1}^{K'} dy_i/y_i$, and
\begin{equation*}
\int F_-\, dm_X^{\otimes K'} \Le \int F\, dm_X^{\otimes K'} \Le \int F_+\, dm_X^{\otimes K'}.
\end{equation*} So $\int F\, dm_X^{\otimes K'}$ tends to $\int F \prod_{i=1}^{K'} dy_i/y_i$, the integral in \eqref{eq: prime tuples}, and \eqref{eq: prime tuples} follows.

Now consider the sum in \eqref{eq: boxes to integrals}. For each tuple $\boldsymbol{\varpi}$ in its range, $S(\mathcal{B}_{\varpi_1 \cdots \varpi_K}, \varpi_K)$ counts the cofactors $t = \varpi'_1 \cdots \varpi'_h$ with $\varpi_K \preccurlyeq \varpi'_1 \preccurlyeq \dots \preccurlyeq \varpi'_h$ and $\varpi_1 \cdots \varpi_K t \in \mathcal{B}$. Here $h \Le 1/c_1$, and $t \neq 1$ because
\begin{equation*}
\prod_{i=1}^{K} |\varpi_i|^2 \Le X^{1 - c_1} < X/2.
\end{equation*} Every element of $\mathcal{B}$ is the product of $\mathcal{O}(1)$ tuples $(\boldsymbol{\varpi}, \varpi'_1, \dots, \varpi'_h)$, since all their primes have norm at least $X^{c_1}$. So the number of tuples in which a prime $p$ is repeated is at most a constant times the number of elements of $\mathcal{B}$ divisible by $p^2$ for some prime $p$ with $|p|^2 \Ge X^{c_1}$, and, by Lemma~\ref{lem: counting}(a),
\begin{equation*}
\sum_{|p|^2 \Ge X^{c_1}} |\mathcal{B}_{p^2}| \Le \sum_{|p|^2 \Ge X^{c_1}} \frac{CX}{|p|^4} \ll X^{1 - c_1}.
\end{equation*} A prime distinct from a prime $p$ and of the same norm is the complex conjugate of $p$, and their product has norm $|p|^4$, so the same bound holds for the tuples that contain distinct primes of equal norm. The remaining tuples have distinct norms. For them, $y_K \Le y_i$ on $\mathcal{R}$ gives $|\varpi_K|^2 < |\varpi_i|^2$, so the condition $\varpi_K \prec \varpi_i$ $(i < K)$ of \eqref{eq: boxes to integrals} holds. Their number is $1/h!$ times the number of such tuples in which $\varpi'_1, \dots, \varpi'_h$ are each $\succ \varpi_K$ and in any order.

Apply \eqref{eq: prime tuples} with $K' = K + h - 1$ and the free prime $\varpi_0 = \varpi'_h$ to the region
\begin{equation*}
\mathcal{R}'_h := \Bigl\{ (\boldsymbol{y}, x_1, \dots, x_{h-1}) : \boldsymbol{y} \in \mathcal{R},\ x_i \Ge y_K \ (1 \Le i \Le h - 1),\ 1 - \sum_{i=1}^{K} y_i - \sum_{i=1}^{h-1} x_i \Ge y_K \Bigr\},
\end{equation*}
where $x_i := \log|\varpi'_i|^2/\log X$ is the exponent of $\varpi'_i$ for $1 \Le i \Le h - 1$, and put
\begin{equation*}
x_h := 1 - \sum_{i=1}^{K} y_i - \sum_{i=1}^{h-1} x_i.
\end{equation*}
Since $|\varpi_1 \cdots \varpi_K t|^2 \in [X/2, X)$, the condition $x_h \Ge y_K$ of $\mathcal{R}'_h$ is the condition $\varpi'_h \succcurlyeq \varpi_K$ in exponents, up to $\mathcal{O}(1/\log X)$. So the number of the tuples with $\varpi'_1, \dots, \varpi'_h$ in any order lies between the values of \eqref{eq: prime tuples} for the regions obtained from $\mathcal{R}'_h$ by moving the boundary of this condition by a fixed amount below $c_1/2$ in either direction. These regions satisfy the hypotheses of \eqref{eq: prime tuples} with $c_1/2$ in place of $c_1$. The integrals over these regions tend to the integral over $\mathcal{R}'_h$ as the amount tends to $0$.

The integrand of \eqref{eq: prime tuples} on $\mathcal{R}'_h$ is $(y_1 \cdots y_K)^{-1} \prod_{i=1}^{h} 1/x_i$, and $\mathcal{R}'_h$ imposes $x_i \Ge y_K$ for $1 \Le i \Le h$. Both are symmetric in $x_1, \dots, x_h$, and so is the measure $dx_1 \cdots dx_{h-1}$, since exchanging $x_h$ and $x_i$ for $i < h$ changes $(x_1, \dots, x_{h-1})$ by an affine map of determinant $-1$. So $1/h!$ of the integral over $\mathcal{R}'_h$ is the integral over the subset with $x_1 \Le \dots \Le x_h$. Hence the sum in \eqref{eq: boxes to integrals} is
\begin{equation*}
\frac{X}{2\log X} \Bigl( \int_{\mathcal{R}} g\Bigl(1 - \sum_{i=1}^{K} y_i;\, y_K\Bigr) \frac{d\boldsymbol{y}}{y_1 \cdots y_K} + o(1) \Bigr),
\end{equation*}
where
\begin{equation*}
g(w; b) := \sum_{h \Ge 1} \int_{b \Le x_1 \Le \dots \Le x_{h-1} \Le w - \sum_{i < h} x_i} \prod_{i < h} \frac{dx_i}{x_i} \cdot \Bigl( w - \sum_{i < h} x_i \Bigr)^{-1}.
\end{equation*}

It remains to show that $g(w; b) = \omega(w/b)/b$, since then the integrand $g(1 - \sum_{i=1}^{K} y_i; y_K)/(y_1 \cdots y_K)$ is that of \eqref{eq: boxes to integrals}. The term $h = 1$ of $g(w; b)$ is $1/w$ for $w \Ge b$. For $h \Ge 2$, the smallest coordinate $x_1$ lies in $[b, w/2]$, and the other coordinates $x_2 \Le \dots \Le x_h$ are at least $x_1$ and sum to $w - x_1$, so they give the term $h - 1$ of $g(w - x_1; x_1)$. Hence
\begin{equation}
\label{eq: g relation}
g(w; b) = \frac{1}{w} + \int_b^{w/2} g(w - x; x)\, \frac{dx}{x} \quad (w \Ge b), \qquad g(w; b) = 0 \quad (w < b).
\end{equation}
For $w \Ge b$, put $\omega((w - x)/x)/x$ in place of $g(w - x; x)$ in \eqref{eq: g relation}. The substitution $x = w/(v + 1)$ and then \eqref{eq: buchstab function} at $u = w/b$ give
\begin{equation*}
\frac{1}{w} + \int_b^{w/2} \omega\Bigl( \frac{w - x}{x} \Bigr) \frac{dx}{x^2} = \frac{1}{w} \Bigl( 1 + \int_1^{w/b - 1} \omega(v)\, dv \Bigr) = \frac{1}{b}\, \omega\Bigl( \frac{w}{b} \Bigr),
\end{equation*}
so $\omega(w/b)/b$ satisfies \eqref{eq: g relation}.
The functions $g(w; b)$ and $\omega(w/b)/b$ both vanish for $w < b$, and \eqref{eq: g relation} determines them by induction on $\lceil w/b \rceil$, since $(w - x)/x \Le w/b - 1$. So $g(w; b) = \omega(w/b)/b$, and \eqref{eq: boxes to integrals} follows from \eqref{eq: primes in B}.
\end{proof}

\section{Inequalities used in the decomposition}
\label{app: inequalities}

Table~\ref{tab: inequalities} lists the inequalities between the parameters \eqref{eq: parameters} that the decomposition uses, directly or through the lemmas of Sections~\ref{sec: estimates}--\ref{sec: two primes}. Each inequality of a row is equivalent to the positivity of the quantity of that row. The quantities are linear in $\theta$ and non-negative at $\theta = 1/9$ and $\theta = 1/8$, hence positive for $1/9 < \theta < 1/8$.

\begin{table}[ht]
\centering

\section{Comparison with the argument of Harman and Lewis}
\label{app: comparison}

Table~\ref{tab: corrections} lists the points where this paper adds to, or differs from, the argument of \citet{harman2001gaussian}. The paragraphs and remarks after the table give the details, in the order of the table.

\begin{remark}
\label{rem: annulus}
Harman and Lewis print, in their (27) and (28), the conditions $ut \in \mathcal{A}$ and $uvt \in \mathcal{A}$, here written in the notation of Lemma~\ref{lem: type I input}. The proof of their Lemma~6 selects the norm range by the factor $(X/|u|^2)^s (1 - 2^{-s})$ in a Perron integral of the Hecke $L$-function $L(s, \lambda^m)$ of \citet[(19)]{harman2001gaussian}, with no condition on the argument, and the proof of their Lemma~7 proceeds in the same way. So the conditions are $ut \in \mathcal{B}$ and $uvt \in \mathcal{B}$, which is also the form needed in Lemma~\ref{lem: sector inequality}. Their stated bound is $X\eta$, where $\eta = (\log X)^{-A}$ with $A$ arbitrarily large \citep[p.~120]{harman2001gaussian}. This bound holds for every $A$, since the proofs of their Lemmas~6 and~7 save a power of $\mu$. The hypothesis $\theta > 1/14$ of their Lemma~7 holds throughout this paper.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}
\label{rem: lemma 4 range}
\citet[Lemma~5 and (30)]{harman2001gaussian} state the bilinear estimate for $U, V \Ge X^{\sigma}$ and $UV \Le X\mu^{-1}$, with the prime $\varpi$ restricted only through the condition $uv\varpi \in \mathcal{E}$. After Perron's formula, the prime in their proof \citep[p.~126]{harman2001gaussian} runs over the norms $|\varpi|^2 \Le 4X/|uv|^2$, and the proof applies their Lemma~4, which is Lemma~\ref{lem: hecke pnt}, with $|t| \Le X$ and $S \Le 4X/(UV)$. As stated, Lemma~\ref{lem: hecke pnt} then requires
\begin{equation*}
S \Ge \exp\bigl( ((1 + \sigma) \log X + A \log\log X)^{4/5} \bigr),
\end{equation*}
and this lower bound is about $\mu^{1.3}$. This requirement is not met when $UV$ is close to $X\mu^{-1}$. Lemma~\ref{lem: type II} stays within the range of Lemma~\ref{lem: hecke pnt}: its prime has norm at least $\mu^4/2$.
\end{remark}

Harman and Lewis invoke a fundamental lemma of sieve theory in the proofs of their Lemmas~8, 9, 11 and~12. Lemma~\ref{lem: fundamental} states the form used in this paper, at levels up to $\mu^4$, and Appendix~\ref{app: sieve} proves it by Brun's pure sieve.

Lemmas~8, 9 and~10 of \citet{harman2001gaussian} give the sums $\sum_n a_n S(\mathcal{E}_n, l(n))$ asymptotic formulas, and Lemmas~\ref{lem: level large} and~\ref{lem: level small} and Corollary~\ref{cor: level one} prove these formulas. The proofs of Harman and Lewis apply the bilinear estimate with a prime of norm down to $\mu$, while Lemma~\ref{lem: type II} keeps the prime at norm at least $\mu^4/2$. For the prime $q$ of the first Buchstab step, they remove the condition $|q|^2 < l(n)$ by Perron's formula \citep[p.~128]{harman2001gaussian}, and the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: level large} removes it by short intervals. Their Lemma~9 iterates Buchstab's identity \eqref{eq: buchstab} up to $(\log X)^{1/5}$ times. The proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: level small} organizes this iteration into stages, with coefficients bounded uniformly in the stage.

Lemmas~8 and~9 of \citet{harman2001gaussian} concern $S$ and coefficients $a_n$ bounded by a divisor function, while Lemmas~\ref{lem: level large} and~\ref{lem: level small} are stated for $S'$ and for $a_n \Ge 0$. The forms with $S$ and with complex $a_n$ follow. For a divisor-bounded $a_n \Ge 0$ and a function $l$,
\begin{equation*}
\sum_n a_n S(\mathcal{E}_n, l(n)) = \sum_n a'_n S'(\mathcal{E}_n, l(n)),
\end{equation*}
where $a'_n := a_n$ if every prime factor of $n$ has norm at least $l(n)$, and $a'_n := 0$ otherwise. A divisor-bounded complex sequence is a combination of $4$ non-negative divisor-bounded sequences.

Lemma~8 of \citet{harman2001gaussian} is the case of Lemma~\ref{lem: level large} with $n \sim Y$ and $l(n) = R|n|^k$, where $R > 0$ is a constant, $-2 \Le k \Le 2$ and $\mu \Le l(n)$.

Lemma~9 of \citet{harman2001gaussian}, for $n \sim Y$ with $X^{\theta} \ll Y \ll X^{\sigma}$ and $l(n) = R|n|^k \ll z(Y)$, follows from Lemmas~\ref{lem: level small} and~\ref{lem: level large}. For the $n$ with $|n|^2 < X^{\sigma}$, Lemma~\ref{lem: level small} applies: $Y \Le |n|^2 < 2Y$ gives $j(|n|^2) \Le j(Y)$ by \eqref{eq: j function}, and then \eqref{eq: z function} gives
\begin{equation*}
z(|n|^2) \Ge \Bigl( \frac{X^{\tau}}{2Y} \Bigr)^{1/(j(Y)+2)} \Ge \frac{z(Y)}{2}.
\end{equation*} For the $n$ with $|n|^2 \Ge X^{\sigma}$, Lemma~\ref{lem: level large} applies, since $z(Y) \Le X^{\tau}/Y \Le 2X^{\tau}/|n|^2$. Lemma~10 of \citet{harman2001gaussian} is Corollary~\ref{cor: level one}.

In the proofs of their Lemmas~11 and~12, Harman and Lewis remove the conditions that couple the variables before applying their bilinear estimate (30), by ``applying Perron's formula to disentangle the relationship between $r$ and $s$'' and by ``removing the interdependence between $s$ and $t$'' \citep[p.~130]{harman2001gaussian}. Lemma~\ref{lem: decoupling} removes them by short intervals.

In their Lemma~11, Harman and Lewis assume
\begin{equation*}
X^{\sigma} \Le P \ll X^{\chi}, \qquad \max(X^{\tau}/P, PX^{-2\theta}) \ll Q \ll X^{\nu}/(P\mu).
\end{equation*}
where $\chi = (34\theta + 7)/24$ \citep[p.~120]{harman2001gaussian}, which equals $(\nu + 2\theta)/2$. Since $\chi < \zeta$ by Table~\ref{tab: inequalities}, their hypotheses imply those of Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 11} on boxes at distance at least $\varepsilon$ from the boundary of the region that these hypotheses define. Their lower bound $X^{\tau}/P$ for $Q$ is not used.

Lemma~12 of \citet{harman2001gaussian} assumes $X^{\sigma} \Le PQ \Le X^{\zeta}\mu^{-1}$, $PQR \Le X^{\nu}\mu^{-1}$ and $P \Ge Q \Ge R \Ge X^{\theta}$, and concerns the sum of $S(\mathcal{A}_{pqr}, X^{\tau}/(PQ))$ over $p \sim P$, $q \sim Q$ and $r \sim R$. The terms of this sum with $|r|^2 < X^{\tau}/(PQ)$ vanish, since $S(\mathcal{A}_{pqr}, X^{\tau}/(PQ))$ then sifts out $r$. So, for $Q \Ge X^{\tau}/(PQ)$, this sum is the sum \eqref{eq: lemma 12 sum} with $\mathcal{I}_q = [Q, 2Q)$ and $\mathcal{I}_r = [\max(R, X^{\tau}/(PQ)), 2R)$, without the constraints $q \prec p$ and $r \prec q$. On boxes at distance at least $\varepsilon$ from the boundary of the region that the hypotheses of their Lemma~12 define, the proof of case (a) of Lemma~\ref{lem: three primes} gives this sum an asymptotic formula, with $|r|^2 < 2R$ and $|pqr|^2 < 8PQR$ in place of $|r|^2 < 2Q$ and $|pqr|^2 < 8PQ^2$. The bound $Q \Le X^{\rho - \varepsilon}$ of case (a) holds, since $Q^2 \Le PQ \Le X^{\zeta}$ and $\zeta < 2\rho$ by Table~\ref{tab: inequalities}. The proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: three primes} applies without the constraints $r \prec q$ and $q \prec p$. The bound $|p|^2 \Ge P \Ge Q = \inf \mathcal{I}_q$ replaces $|p|^2 \Ge |q|^2$ where that proof shows that every prime factor of $pqr$ has norm at least $X^{\tau}/(PQ)$. Dropping a constraint changes none of its other checks.

Their hypothesis $R \Ge X^{\theta}$ is not used in the proof of Lemma~\ref{lem: three primes}. In their Lemma~13, where their Lemma~12 is applied, $Q^2P \Ge X^{\tau}$ and $PQ \Le X^{\zeta}\mu^{-1}$, and every $r$ has $|r|^2 \Ge X^{\tau}/(PQ)$. Since $r \sim R$ and $\tau - \zeta - \theta = (1 - 8\theta)/6$ is positive, the bound $PQ \Le X^{\zeta}\mu^{-1}$ gives
\begin{equation*}
R > \frac{|r|^2}{2} \Ge \frac{X^{\tau}}{2PQ} \Ge \frac{X^{\tau - \zeta}\mu}{2} > X^{\theta}.
\end{equation*} The decomposition of Section~\ref{sec: decomposition} applies Lemma~\ref{lem: three primes}, through Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 13}, on boxes of $\mathsf{R}$ with $PQ > X^{1/2}$, where $X^{\tau}/(PQ) < X^{\theta}$, so primes $r$ with $|r|^2 < X^{\theta}$ occur.

Lemma~13 of \citet{harman2001gaussian} is case (a) of Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 13}. Since $|q|^2 < 2Q$, the sum $S_\Ge$ of \eqref{eq: lemma 13 split} vanishes when $2PQ^2 \Le X^{\tau}$, and their condition $Q^2P < X^{\tau}$ is this case up to the factor $2$. Case (a) of Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 13} also needs $Q \Le X^{\rho - \varepsilon}$, which is not among their hypotheses. Their hypotheses imply it for small $\varepsilon$, as for their Lemma~12. The proof of their Lemma~13 cites their Lemmas~8 and~12. The sum over the pairs with $|q|^2 < X^{\tau}/(PQ)$ has the prime level $q$, which is not a level $R|pq|^k$ of their Lemma~8. Lemmas~\ref{lem: level large} and~\ref{lem: decoupling} of this paper allow this level. Case (b) of Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 13} has no counterpart in their Lemma~13. Section~\ref{sec: outline} describes its use on $\mathsf{R}$.

Part~6 of \citet[\S5]{harman2001gaussian} uses the case $j(PQ) = 1$ of Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 14}, which is stated and proved here. For $j(PQ) = 0$, Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 14} is Lemma~14 and (36) of \citet{harman2001gaussian}. Then $z(PQ) = (X^{\tau}/(PQ))^{1/2}$ by \eqref{eq: z function}, and, since $|q|^2 < 2Q$, the sum $S_\Ge$ of \eqref{eq: lemma 14 split} vanishes when $4PQ^3 \Le X^{\tau}$. Their condition $PQ^3 < X^{\tau}$ is this case up to the factor $4$.

\begin{remark}
\label{rem: second term 37}
\citet[(37)]{harman2001gaussian} sum $S(\mathcal{E}_p, z(|p|^2))$ over all $p$ with $X^{\theta} \Le |p|^2 < X^{1/2}$. For $p \in \Pi_2$, \eqref{eq: Pi} gives $z(|p|^2) > |p|^2$, so $S(\mathcal{E}_p, z(|p|^2))$ sifts $p$ itself and vanishes, while the term $S(\mathcal{E}_p, p)$ of the first Buchstab step remains. Identity \eqref{eq: identity 37} keeps the sum of $S(\mathcal{E}_p, p)$ over $p \in \Pi_2$, and Lemma~\ref{lem: identity 37} gives it an asymptotic formula.
\end{remark}

The primes with $\tau/4 \Le \alpha < \sigma - 2\theta$ exist only for $\theta < 3/26$, so they do not occur in the decomposition of \citet[\S5]{harman2001gaussian} at $\theta = 0.119$. Section~\ref{sec: decomposition} treats them in the paragraph after \eqref{eq: four dim regions}.

\begin{remark}
\label{rem: misprints}
The regions of Section~\ref{sec: decomposition} are derived from the lemmas. Of these regions, those for $\varphi_2$ and $\varphi_{10}$ differ from the regions that Harman and Lewis print. \citet[p.~132]{harman2001gaussian} print the lower limit $\zeta - \alpha$ for $\beta$ in $\varphi_2$. For $\alpha > (1 + 10\theta)/6$, this limit lies below $\beta_0(\alpha) = (\tau - \alpha)/2$ of \eqref{eq: beta0}, and the strip between these limits contains no pair of $\sum^*$. Harman and Lewis print the upper limit $1/2 - 2\theta - \alpha$ for $\beta$ in $\varphi_{10}$, which makes the region empty for every $\alpha \Ge \tau/4$. The condition $|pq|^2 < X^{\sigma}$ of Lemma~\ref{lem: lemma 14} with $j(PQ) = 0$ gives the upper limit $\sigma - \alpha$. They also print $\chi = 0.46205$ at $\theta = 0.119$ \citep[p.~120]{harman2001gaussian}, while $(34\theta + 7)/24 = 0.46025$.
\end{remark}

Harman and Lewis discard the third sums of \eqref{eq: identity 35} and \eqref{eq: identity 36}, except on a few subregions of the regions of $\varphi_3$, $\varphi_7$ and $\varphi_9$ that they remove by further inequalities, the cuts of Table~\ref{tab: corrections}. They print a grouping of the kind that defines $\mathsf{G}$ and $\mathsf{L}$ only in their $\varphi_7$ \citep[p.~133]{harman2001gaussian}. There, the condition $\delta > 1 - \alpha - \gamma - \sigma$ when $\alpha + \gamma \Ge \sigma$ excludes from the region of $\varphi_7$ the subset of $\mathsf{G}$ given by $\mathcal{S} = \{p, r\}$. This exclusion changes no loss once $\mathsf{G}$ is added, and the evaluation of the decomposition of Harman and Lewis in Section~\ref{sec: numerics} keeps it. Their cut $\gamma \Le \alpha + \beta - 2\theta$ in $\varphi_3$ and $\varphi_9$ is implied by the limits of $\delta$. On $\mathsf{E}_3$ and $\mathsf{E}_9$, $\delta \Ge \tau - \alpha - \beta$ by \eqref{eq: four dim regions}, and the integrand of $\Phi(\mathsf{F}')$ in \eqref{eq: loss} vanishes unless $\delta \Le (1 - \alpha - \beta - \gamma)/2$. These bounds are compatible only if
\begin{equation*}
\gamma \Le 1 - 2\tau + \alpha + \beta = \alpha + \beta - 2\theta.
\end{equation*}

At $\theta = 0.119$, the bounds that Harman and Lewis print for $\varphi_2$, $\varphi_5$ and $\varphi_9$ lie below the losses of Table~\ref{tab: hl 0119}. For $\varphi_9$ they ``omit here a couple of extra conditions on the integral ranges, corresponding to other ways of combining variables to get asymptotic formulae'' \citep[p.~133]{harman2001gaussian}, and the loss of $\mathsf{E}_9$ in Table~\ref{tab: hl 0119} is that of the region they print.
